<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:three-regime}
Let $N\ge3$, $p>1$ and $2p<\sigma<N$, and assume that $q:=p^*_{\sigma}>1$. Let $u$ be a positive $D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$-weak solution of \eqref{eq:main}. Then exactly one of the following alternatives occurs:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item If $\sigma(p-2)+2p>0$ (equivalently $\Lambda>0$), then
\[
u\simeq |x|^{-\gamma_h},\qquad |\nabla u|\simeq |x|^{-\gamma_h-1},
\]
and $u$ is radially symmetric about some point and strictly decreasing in the radial variable.
\item If $\sigma(p-2)+2p=0$ (equivalently $\Lambda=0$), then
\[
u\simeq |x|^{-\gamma_h}(\log|x|)^{1/(p-q)},
\qquad
|\nabla u|\simeq |x|^{-\gamma_h-1}(\log|x|)^{1/(p-q)},
\]
and $u$ is radially symmetric about some point and strictly decreasing in the radial variable.
\item If $\sigma(p-2)+2p<0$ (equivalently $\Lambda<0$), then, with $m_0=\int_{\mathbb R^N}u^q$ and
$A_*=(m_0/h(\gamma_*))^{1/(p-q)}$,
\[
r^{\gamma_*}u(r\omega)\to A_*,
\qquad
r^{\gamma_*+1}\nabla u(r\omega)\to-A_*\gamma_*\omega
\]
uniformly for $\omega\in S^{N-1}$. In particular
\[
u\simeq |x|^{-\gamma_*},
\qquad
|\nabla u|\simeq |x|^{-\gamma_*-1},
\qquad \gamma_*<\gamma_h,
\]
and $u$ is radially symmetric about some point and strictly decreasing in the radial variable.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:local-boundedness}
Let $N\ge3$, $p>1$ and $2p<\sigma<N$, and assume that $q:=p^*_{\sigma}>1$. Then every nonnegative $D^{1,p}(\mathbb R^N)$-weak solution of \eqref{eq:main} belongs to $L^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^N)$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:exponents}
Let $N\ge3$, $p>1$ and $2p<\sigma<N$, and let $q,\gamma_{h},\gamma_{e},\gamma_{*},\Lambda$ be as in \eqref{eq:psigma}, \eqref{eq:gammas} and \eqref{eq:Lambda}. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item $p^{*}/2<q<p$ and $2q>p$;
\item $\gamma_{e}<\gamma_{h}$ and $\gamma_{*}>\gamma_{e}$;
\item $\Lambda>0\iff\sigma(2-p)<2p\iff\gamma_{*}>\gamma_{h}$, and $\Lambda>0$ holds whenever $p\ge2$;
\item $\sigma-(p-q)\gamma_{h}=p+\Lambda$ and $q\gamma_{h}+\sigma-N-(p-q)\gamma_{h}=p+\frac{N+p-\sigma}{p-1}$;
\item $q\gamma_{h}>N$ if and only if $\sigma<2N/p$; moreover if $\Lambda\le0$ then $\sigma<2N/p$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
(a) From \eqref{eq:psigma}, $q-p^{*}/2=p(N-\sigma)/(2(N-p))>0$ and $q-p^{*}=-p\sigma/(2(N-p))<0$, while $q-p<0$ by \eqref{eq:q-minus-p} and $\sigma>2p$. Since $q>p^{*}/2>p/2$ we get $2q>p$.

(b) The first is clear from $p-1<p$. For the second, $\gamma_{*}-\gamma_{e}=\sigma(p-N)/\big(p(2p-\sigma)\big)>0$ because $p<N$ and $\sigma>2p$.

(c) A computation gives
\[
\gamma_{*}-\gamma_{h}=\frac{(p-N)\big(p\sigma+2p-2\sigma\big)}{p(p-1)(2p-\sigma)} ,
\]
and both $p-N$ and $2p-\sigma$ are negative, so the sign of $\gamma_{*}-\gamma_{h}$ is that of $2p-\sigma(2-p)$. The identity $\sigma+(q-1)\gamma_{h}-N=(\sigma(p-2)+2p)/(2(p-1))$ is a direct computation from \eqref{eq:psigma}, and shows that $\Lambda$ has the same sign. If $p\ge2$ then $\sigma(2-p)\le0<2p$.

(d) Both identities follow from $p-q=p(\sigma-2p)/(2(N-p))$ and $\gamma_{h}=(N-p)/(p-1)$.

(e) $q\gamma_{h}-N=(N-p\sigma/2)/(p-1)$, which is positive if and only if $\sigma<2N/p$. If $\Lambda\le0$ then $p<2$ by (c), whence $2N/p>N>\sigma$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:qbigger1}
Under the assumptions of Lemma \ref{lem:exponents} together with $\Lambda\ge0$, one has $q>1$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
From \eqref{eq:psigma}, $q>1$ is equivalent to $\sigma<2N(p-1)/p+2$. Suppose first that $p\ge2N/(N+2)$. Then $p(N-2)\le2N(p-1)$, that is, $N\le2N(p-1)/p+2$, and the claim follows from $\sigma<N$. Suppose next that $p<2N/(N+2)$, so in particular $p<2$. Then $2p<N(2-p)$, hence $2/(2-p)<N/p$, hence
\[
\frac{2p}{2-p}-2=\frac{4(p-1)}{2-p}<\frac{2N(p-1)}{p},
\]
so $2p/(2-p)\le2N(p-1)/p+2$. Since $\Lambda\ge0$ gives $\sigma\le2p/(2-p)$ by Lemma \ref{lem:exponents}(c), while the preceding inequality is strict in the present case, the claim follows.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:conv}
Let $0<\sigma<N$, $q>0$ and $R>0$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item If $u\ge0$ in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ and $u\ge C_{1}|x|^{-\beta}$ in $\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}$ with $C_{1},\beta>0$ and $q\beta>N-\sigma$, then $|x|^{-\sigma}*u^{q}\ge C|x|^{N-\sigma-q\beta}$ in $\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}$.
\item If $u\in L^{\infty}(B_{R})$ and $0\le u\le C_{1}|x|^{-\beta}$ in $\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{R}$ with $q\beta>N-\sigma$, then in $\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus B_{2R}$,
\[
|x|^{-\sigma}*u^{q}\le
\begin{cases}
C|x|^{N-\sigma-q\beta} &\text{if }N-\sigma<q\beta<N,\\
C|x|^{-\sigma}\log|x| &\text{if }q\beta=N,\\
C|x|^{-\sigma} &\text{if }q\beta>N.
\end{cases}
\]
\item If $c(1+|x|^{\beta})^{-1}\le f\le C(1+|x|^{\beta})^{-1}$ in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ with $\beta>N-\sigma$, then $C_{1}\le|x|^{-\sigma}*f\le C_{2}$ in $B_{R}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:rhs-local}
Let $q>1$ and let $u$ be a positive locally bounded $D^{1,p}(\mathbb R^N)$-weak solution of \eqref{eq:main}. Then, for every compact set $K\subset\mathbb R^N$,
\[
W[u]\in W^{1,\infty}(K),\qquad
f:=W[u]u^{q-1}\in W^{1,\infty}(K),\qquad
\inf_K f>0.
\]
In particular, if $\alpha$ is a local $C^{1,\alpha}$ exponent furnished by the interior regularity theory, then one may choose any
\[
0<\mu<\min\{\alpha(p-1),\tfrac12\}
\]
so that the local assumptions (2.7)--(2.9), including condition $(I_\alpha)$, in \cite[Remark 2.3, Lemma 2.9]{DaiLiLiu2025} hold on every bounded subdomain compactly contained in $\mathbb R^N$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Interior regularity gives $u\in C^{1,\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^N)$. Fix a compact set $K\subset\mathbb R^N$ and choose $\chi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^N)$ with $\chi=1$ on a neighborhood of $K$. Write
\[
W[u](x)=\int_{\mathbb R^N}|x-y|^{-\sigma}\chi(y)u(y)^q\,dy
      +\int_{\mathbb R^N}|x-y|^{-\sigma}(1-\chi(y))u(y)^q\,dy
      =:W_{\rm near}(x)+W_{\rm far}(x).
\]
Since $|x|^{-\sigma}\in L^1_{\rm loc}(\mathbb R^N)$ and $\chi u^q\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^N)$, differentiation in the sense of distributions and integration by parts give
\[
\partial_iW_{\rm near}=|\cdot|^{-\sigma}*\partial_i(\chi u^q),
\]
which is bounded on $K$. For the far part, set $\delta:=\operatorname{dist}(K,\operatorname{supp}(1-\chi))>0$. The kernel may then be differentiated directly. With
\[
a:=\frac{p^*}{q}=\frac{2N}{2N-\sigma},
\qquad a'=\frac{2N}{\sigma},
\]
H\"older's inequality yields, uniformly for $x\in K$,
\[
|\nabla W_{\rm far}(x)|
\le C\|u^q\|_{L^a}
\left(\int_{|x-y|\ge\delta}|x-y|^{-(\sigma+1)a'}\,dy\right)^{1/a'}<\infty,
\]
because $(\sigma+1)a'>N$. Thus $W[u]\in W^{1,\infty}(K)$. Since $u$ is positive and continuous, it is bounded below by a positive constant on $K$; moreover $W[u]$ is positive and continuous because the kernel is positive and $u\not\equiv0$. Hence $\inf_KW[u]>0$. The assertions for $f=W[u]u^{q-1}$ follow from the product rule, using again the positive lower bound for $u$ on $K$ when $1<q<2$.

It remains only to match precisely the local lower-bound assumption used in \cite[Lemma 2.9]{DaiLiLiu2025}. Let $\Omega$ be a bounded domain with compact closure in $\mathbb R^N$, choose a compact $K$ whose interior contains $\overline\Omega$, and fix
$0<\mu<\min\{\alpha(p-1),\tfrac12\}$. Since $c_K:=\inf_K f>0$, for every $x_0\in\Omega$ and every ball $B_\rho(x_0)\subset K$ one has
\[
f(x)\ge c_K\ge c_K\rho^{-\mu}|x-x_0|^\mu
\qquad (x\in B_\rho(x_0)).
\]
Moreover $f\in W^{1,\infty}(K)$, hence $f\in W^{1,m}(\Omega)$ for every finite $m$, in particular for some
$m>N/[2(1-\mu)]$, and also $f\in L^s(\Omega)$ for every $s<\infty$. Thus (2.7)--(2.9) and condition $(I_\alpha)$ of \cite[Remark 2.3]{DaiLiLiu2025} are satisfied.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:weighted-sobolev-local}
Assume $p\ge2$ and let $u$ be a positive locally bounded $D^{1,p}(\mathbb R^N)$-weak solution of \eqref{eq:main}. For every ball $B$ with compact closure in $\mathbb R^N$ there exist an exponent $r_B>2$ and a constant $C_B>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weighted-sobolev-local}
\|\phi\|_{L^{r_B}(B)}^2\le C_B\int_B |\nabla u|^{p-2}|\nabla\phi|^2\,dx
\qquad\text{for every }\phi\in C_c^1(B).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
If $p=2$, the weight is identically one and \eqref{eq:weighted-sobolev-local} is the usual Sobolev inequality, with any $2<r_B\le2N/(N-2)$.

Assume $p>2$, and choose a slightly larger ball $\widetilde B$ with $\overline B\subset\widetilde B$ and $\overline{\widetilde B}\subset\mathbb R^N$. By Lemma \ref{lem:rhs-local}, the Hartree right-hand side $f=W[u]u^{q-1}$ satisfies on $\widetilde B$ the local assumptions used in \cite[Lemma 2.9]{DaiLiLiu2025}. Hence the inverse-gradient estimate (2.13) of that lemma and the finite-covering argument of \cite[Corollary 2.10]{DaiLiLiu2025} give the local estimate (2.14): for every
\[
1\le t<\frac{p-1}{p-2},\qquad 0\le\gamma<N-2,
\]
the quantity
\[
\sup_{x\in B}\int_B
\frac{dy}{|\nabla u(y)|^{(p-2)t}|x-y|^{\gamma}}
\]
is finite, after enlarging the constant if necessary.

Fix
\[
1<t<\frac{p-1}{p-2}
\]
and then choose
\[
\max\{0,N-2t\}<\gamma<N-2.
\]
This is possible because $t>1$. With $\varrho=|\nabla u|^{p-2}$ we therefore have
\[
\sup_{x\in B}\int_B\varrho(y)^{-t}|x-y|^{-\gamma}\,dy<\infty,
\qquad
\gamma<N,
\qquad
t>\frac{N-\gamma}{2},
\qquad
2>1+\frac1t.
\]
These are precisely the abstract hypotheses singled out in \cite[Remark 3.1]{DamascelliSciunzi2006} for the weighted Sobolev and Poincar\'e inequalities proved there; see also \cite[Theorem 2.2]{DamascelliSciunzi2006}. Applying that weighted Sobolev inequality on the fixed ball $B$ yields \eqref{eq:weighted-sobolev-local} for some exponent $r_B>2$. Notice that only the existence of one such exponent is used below.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:comparison}
Let $\Omega$ be either a bounded open subset of $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ or the exterior domain $\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus\overline{B_{\rho}}$ with $\rho>0$, and let $u,v\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\overline{\Omega})$ be such that $(u-v)^{+}\in D^{1,p}(\Omega)$ and $(u-v)^{+}=0$ on $\partial\Omega$. In the exterior-domain case assume in addition that $\nabla u,\nabla v\in L^{p}(\Omega)$. If
\[
\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\langle\nabla u,\nabla\varphi\rangle\,dx
\le\int_{\Omega}|\nabla v|^{p-2}\langle\nabla v,\nabla\varphi\rangle\,dx
\]
for every nonnegative $\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)$, then $u\le v$ in $\Omega$.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
By density the inequality holds for every nonnegative $\varphi\in D^{1,p}(\Omega)$ with compact support in $\overline{\Omega}$ and $\varphi=0$ on $\partial\Omega$. If $\Omega$ is bounded this already covers $\varphi=(u-v)^{+}$ and we may pass directly to the display below. In the exterior case, let $\eta_{R}$ be cut-offs with $\eta_{R}=1$ on $B_{R}$, $\eta_{R}=0$ outside $B_{2R}$ and $|\nabla\eta_{R}|\le C/R$, and put $\varphi=(u-v)^{+}\eta_{R}$. Writing $w=(u-v)^{+}$ and $A_{R}=B_{2R}\setminus B_{R}$, the error committed in replacing $\varphi$ by $w$ is controlled by
\[
\frac{C}{R}\int_{A_{R}}\big(|\nabla u|^{p-1}+|\nabla v|^{p-1}\big)w\,dx
\le C\big\|\nabla u\big\|^{p-1}_{L^{p}(A_{R})}\|w\|_{L^{p^{*}}(A_{R})}+\cdots,
\]
where we used H\"older's inequality and $R^{-1}|A_{R}|^{\frac1p-\frac{1}{p^{*}}}\le C$, the omitted term being the same expression with $\nabla u$ replaced by $\nabla v$. Both terms tend to $0$ as $R\to\infty$ because $\nabla u,\nabla v\in L^{p}(\Omega)$ and $w\in L^{p^{*}}(\Omega)$. Hence the inequality holds for $\varphi=w$. Testing with $\varphi=(u-v)^{+}$ gives
\[
\int_{\Omega}\langle|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u-|\nabla v|^{p-2}\nabla v,\nabla(u-v)^{+}\rangle\,dx\le0 .
\]
The integrand is nonnegative and vanishes only where $\nabla(u-v)^{+}=0$, so $(u-v)^{+}$ is constant, and it belongs to $D^{1,p}(\Omega)$, so it vanishes.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:positivity-alternative}
Let $N\ge3$, $p>1$ and $2p<\sigma<N$, and assume that $q:=p^*_{\sigma}>1$. Let $u$ be a nonnegative $D^{1,p}(\mathbb R^N)$-weak solution of \eqref{eq:main}. Then either $u\equiv0$ a.e. in $\mathbb R^N$ or $u>0$ a.e. in $\mathbb R^N$. In the latter case the continuous representative of $u$ on $\mathbb R^N\setminus S$ is strictly positive at every point.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Since the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:main} is nonnegative, $u$ is a nonnegative weak supersolution of $-\Delta_pu\ge0$. The weak Harnack inequality for nonnegative $p$-supersolutions implies that on concentric balls $\overline{B_r}\subset B_{2r}$,
\[
\left(\frac1{|B_{2r}|}\int_{B_{2r}}u^\theta\right)^{1/\theta}\le C\,\operatorname*{ess\,inf}_{B_r}u
\]
for some $\theta>0$. If $u\not\equiv0$, one ball has positive $L^\theta$ mass, hence $u$ has positive essential infimum on a smaller ball. A chain of overlapping balls propagates this positivity through the connected space $\mathbb R^N$, giving $u>0$ a.e. Finally, on $\mathbb R^N\setminus S$ the local $C^{1,\alpha}$ representative furnished by interior regularity agrees a.e. with $u$, so the same local weak-Harnack estimate gives pointwise positivity there.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:structure}
Let $N\ge3$, $p>1$ and $2p<\sigma<N$, and assume that $q:=p^*_{\sigma}>1$. Let $u$ be a nonnegative $D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$-weak solution of \eqref{eq:main}. Then $u\in C^{1,\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus S)$ for some $0<\alpha<\min\{1,\frac{1}{p-1}\}$, and there is $\Lambda_{0}=\Lambda_{0}(u)<\infty$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:interior}
u(y)\,\min\{d(y),1\}^{\gamma_{e}}\le\Lambda_{0}
\qquad\text{for every }y\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus S .
\end{equation}
Moreover
\begin{equation}\label{eq:vanish}
u(y)\,d(y)^{\gamma_{e}}\longrightarrow0
\qquad\text{as }d(y)\to0 .
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:structure}
Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:structure}, suppose $S=\emptyset$, that is, $u\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$. Then
\[
u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),
\qquad
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\le\Lambda_{0},
\qquad
u(x)\longrightarrow0\ \text{ as }|x|\to\infty .
\]
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:Wloc}
Let $u\in L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$ be nonnegative, let $x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$ and $0<r\le1$, and suppose $u\in L^{\infty}(B_{2r}(x_{0}))$. Then
\[
\|W\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r}(x_{0}))}
\le C\Big(\|u\|^{q}_{L^{\infty}(B_{2r}(x_{0}))}\,r^{N-\sigma}
+\|u\|^{q}_{L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\,r^{-\sigma/2}\Big),
\]
with $C=C(N,\sigma,q)$. In particular $W\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus S)$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Fix $x\in B_{r}(x_{0})$ and split $W(x)=W_{1}(x)+W_{2}(x)$ according to $|x-y|<r$ and $|x-y|\ge r$. If $|x-y|<r$ then $y\in B_{2r}(x_{0})$, so
\[
W_{1}(x)\le\|u\|^{q}_{L^{\infty}(B_{2r}(x_{0}))}\int_{|z|<r}|z|^{-\sigma}dz
=C\|u\|^{q}_{L^{\infty}(B_{2r}(x_{0}))}r^{N-\sigma},
\]
the integral being finite because $\sigma<N$. For $W_{2}$, H\"older's inequality with the exponents $p^{*}/q$ and $2N/\sigma$, which are conjugate because $q/p^{*}=(2N-\sigma)/(2N)$, gives
\[
W_{2}(x)\le\|u\|^{q}_{L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\Big(\int_{|z|\ge r}|z|^{-2N}dz\Big)^{\frac{\sigma}{2N}}
=C\|u\|^{q}_{L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}r^{-\sigma/2}.
\]
Both bounds are independent of $x\in B_{r}(x_{0})$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:regoffS}
We have $u\in C^{1,\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus S)$ for some $0<\alpha<\min\{1,\frac1{p-1}\}$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Let $x_{0}\notin S$, so $u\in L^{\infty}(B_{2r}(x_{0}))$ for some $0<r\le1$. By Lemma \ref{lem:Wloc}, $W\in L^{\infty}(B_{r}(x_{0}))$, hence $Wu^{q-1}\in L^{\infty}(B_{r}(x_{0}))$ because $q>1$. The regularity results of \cite{DiBenedetto1983,Tolksdorf1984} apply.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:scaling}
If $u$ solves \eqref{eq:main}, so does $u_{y,\lambda}$, and for every $R>0$
\begin{equation}\label{eq:scaleinv}
\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla u_{y,\lambda}|^{p}dz=\int_{B_{\lambda R}(y)}|\nabla u|^{p}dx,
\qquad
\int_{B_{R}}u_{y,\lambda}^{p^{*}}dz=\int_{B_{\lambda R}(y)}u^{p^{*}}dx .
\end{equation}
In particular $\|u_{y,\lambda}\|_{L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|u\|_{L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}$.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
A change of variables gives $W[u_{y,\lambda}](z)=\lambda^{q\gamma_{e}-N+\sigma}W[u](y+\lambda z)$, so the right hand side of \eqref{eq:main} evaluated at $u_{y,\lambda}$ equals $\lambda^{(2q-1)\gamma_{e}+\sigma-N}\,(W[u]u^{q-1})(y+\lambda z)$, while $-\Delta_{p}u_{y,\lambda}=\lambda^{\gamma_{e}(p-1)+p}(-\Delta_{p}u)(y+\lambda z)$. The two exponents agree because $\gamma_{e}(2q-p)=N+p-\sigma$, which is the definition of $\gamma_{e}$ rewritten. The identities \eqref{eq:scaleinv} follow from $(\gamma_{e}+1)p=N$ and $\gamma_{e}p^{*}=N$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:doubling}
Let $(X,\rho)$ be a complete metric space, let $\emptyset\ne D\subset\Sigma\subset X$ with $\Sigma$ closed, and set $\Gamma:=\Sigma\setminus D$. Let $M:D\to(0,\infty)$ be bounded on compact subsets of $D$, and let $k>0$. If $y_{0}\in D$ satisfies $M(y_{0})\,\mathrm{dist}(y_{0},\Gamma)>2k$, then there is $x\in D$ with
\[
M(x)\,\mathrm{dist}(x,\Gamma)>2k,
\qquad
M(x)\ge M(y_{0}),
\qquad
M(z)\le2M(x)\ \text{ for all }z\in D\cap \overline{B_{k/M(x)}(x)} .
\]
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:unifsmall}
Let $u\in L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$. Then $\omega(\rho):=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}}\int_{B_{\rho}(x)}u^{p^{*}}dy\to0$ as $\rho\to0$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Given $\varepsilon>0$, pick $g\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$ with $\|u-g\|_{L^{p^{*}}}^{p^{*}}<\varepsilon$. Then $\int_{B_{\rho}(x)}u^{p^{*}}\le2^{p^{*}}(\varepsilon+\|g\|^{p^{*}}_{\infty}|B_{\rho}|)$ for every $x$, and the last term tends to $0$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
The regularity is Lemma \ref{lem:regoffS}. If $u\equiv0$, both \eqref{eq:interior} and \eqref{eq:vanish} are immediate. Hence assume $u\not\equiv0$. By Lemma \ref{lem:positivity-alternative}, the continuous representative of $u$ is strictly positive on $D:=\mathbb R^N\setminus S$.

\emph{Step 1: the interior bound.} Set $M:=u^{1/\gamma_{e}}$. Then $M:D\to(0,\infty)$ is continuous by Lemma \ref{lem:regoffS}, hence bounded on compact subsets of $D$. Suppose, for contradiction, that
\[
\sup_{y\in D}\ M(y)\,\min\{\mathrm{dist}(y,S),1\}=\infty .
\]
Fix $k\in\mathbb{N}$ and choose $y_k\in D$ so that
\[
M(y_k)\min\{\operatorname{dist}(y_k,S),1\}>2k.
\]
Set
\[
\Sigma_k:=\overline{B_1(y_k)},\qquad
D_k:=B_1(y_k)\setminus S,
\qquad
\Gamma_k:=\Sigma_k\setminus D_k=(S\cap\Sigma_k)\cup\partial B_1(y_k).
\]
Then $\Sigma_k$ is closed, $y_k\in D_k$, and
\[
\operatorname{dist}(y_k,\Gamma_k)
=\min\{\operatorname{dist}(y_k,S),1\},
\]
so $M(y_k)\operatorname{dist}(y_k,\Gamma_k)>2k$. Lemma \ref{lem:doubling} produces $x_{k}\in D_{k}$ with
\[
M(x_{k})\,\mathrm{dist}(x_{k},\Gamma_{k})>2k,
\qquad
M(z)\le2M(x_{k})\ \text{ for }z\in D_{k}\cap \overline{B_{k/M(x_{k})}(x_{k})} .
\]
Since $\mathrm{dist}(x_{k},\Gamma_{k})\le1$ we get $\lambda_{k}:=1/M(x_{k})<1/(2k)\to0$, and $\overline{B_{k\lambda_{k}}(x_{k})}\subset D_{k}$.

Let $v_{k}:=u_{x_{k},\lambda_{k}}$ as in \eqref{eq:scaling}. Then $v_{k}$ solves \eqref{eq:main}, and
\[
v_{k}(0)=\lambda_{k}^{\gamma_{e}}u(x_{k})=1,
\qquad
0\le v_{k}(z)=\lambda_{k}^{\gamma_{e}}u(x_{k}+\lambda_{k}z)\le2^{\gamma_{e}}
\quad\text{for }|z|\le k .
\]
By Lemma \ref{lem:Wloc} applied to $v_{k}$ with $r=1$ and centers in $B_{k-2}$, and by $\|v_{k}\|_{L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|u\|_{L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}$ from Lemma \ref{lem:scaling}, the potentials $W[v_{k}]$ are bounded on $B_{k-2}$ by a constant independent of $k$. Since $q>1$ and $\|v_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{k})}\le2^{\gamma_{e}}$, the right hand sides $W[v_{k}]v_{k}^{q-1}$ are bounded in $L^{\infty}(B_{k-2})$ uniformly in $k$, so \cite{DiBenedetto1983,Tolksdorf1984} give, for each fixed $R>0$, a bound for $\|v_{k}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{B_{R}})}$ independent of $k$.

On the other hand, by \eqref{eq:scaleinv} and Lemma \ref{lem:unifsmall},
\[
\int_{B_{R}}v_{k}^{p^{*}}dz=\int_{B_{R\lambda_{k}}(x_{k})}u^{p^{*}}dx\le\omega(R\lambda_{k})\longrightarrow0
\qquad(k\to\infty)
\]
for every fixed $R$. By the Arzel\`a--Ascoli theorem and a diagonal argument, a subsequence of $(v_{k})$ converges in $C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$ to some $v_{\infty}\ge0$ with $v_{\infty}(0)=1$. Fatou's lemma then gives $\int_{B_{R}}v_{\infty}^{p^{*}}=0$ for every $R$, so $v_{\infty}\equiv0$, contradicting $v_{\infty}(0)=1$. This proves \eqref{eq:interior} with some finite $\Lambda_{0}$.

\emph{Step 2: the vanishing factor.} Suppose \eqref{eq:vanish} fails. Then there are $\delta>0$ and $y_{j}\in D$ with $d_{j}:=d(y_{j})\to0$ and $u(y_{j})d_{j}^{\gamma_{e}}\ge\delta$. Put $\lambda_{j}:=d_{j}/2$ and $v_{j}:=u_{y_{j},\lambda_{j}}$. For $|z|\le1$ we have $\mathrm{dist}(y_{j}+\lambda_{j}z,S)\ge d_{j}-\lambda_{j}=\lambda_{j}$, so \eqref{eq:interior} gives $u(y_{j}+\lambda_{j}z)\le\Lambda_{0}\lambda_{j}^{-\gamma_{e}}$ for $j$ large, that is, $v_{j}\le\Lambda_{0}$ on $B_{1}$. Exactly as in Step 1, the $v_{j}$ are bounded in $C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{B_{1/2}})$, while
\[
\int_{B_{1}}v_{j}^{p^{*}}dz=\int_{B_{\lambda_{j}}(y_{j})}u^{p^{*}}dx\le\omega(\lambda_{j})\to0 .
\]
A subsequence converges uniformly on $\overline{B_{1/2}}$ to a continuous $v_{\infty}\ge0$ with $\int_{B_{1/2}}v_{\infty}^{p^{*}}=0$, hence $v_{\infty}\equiv0$. But $v_{j}(0)=\lambda_{j}^{\gamma_{e}}u(y_{j})=2^{-\gamma_{e}}d_{j}^{\gamma_{e}}u(y_{j})\ge2^{-\gamma_{e}}\delta$, a contradiction.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Assume $S=\emptyset$, so $d\equiv+\infty$, $\min\{d,1\}\equiv1$, and \eqref{eq:interior} reads $u\le\Lambda_{0}$ on $\mathbb{R}^{N}$. Hence $u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$. Lemma \ref{lem:Wloc}, applied with $r=1$ and arbitrary centers, gives $W\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$; since $q>1$, $Wu^{q-1}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$, and therefore $u\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$ by \cite{DiBenedetto1983,Tolksdorf1984}.

For the decay, suppose $|y_{j}|\to\infty$ and $u(y_{j})\ge\delta>0$. The translates $v_{j}:=u(y_{j}+\cdot)$ solve \eqref{eq:main}, since $W[v_{j}](z)=W[u](y_{j}+z)$, and they satisfy $\|v_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}\le\Lambda_{0}$ and $\|v_{j}\|_{L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\|u\|_{L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}$. As in Step 1 of the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:structure}, $(v_{j})$ is bounded in $C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{B_{1/2}})$, while
\[
\int_{B_{1}}v_{j}^{p^{*}}dz=\int_{B_{1}(y_{j})}u^{p^{*}}dx\longrightarrow0
\]
because $u\in L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$ and the balls escape to infinity. A subsequence converges uniformly on $\overline{B_{1/2}}$ to a continuous limit with vanishing $L^{p^{*}}$ norm, hence to $0$, contradicting $v_{j}(0)\ge\delta$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:frozen}
Let $p>1$ and $2p<\sigma<N$, and let $q$ be as in \eqref{eq:psigma}. For every $a\in(p,\sigma/2)$ put
\[
\kappa:=\frac{a-p}{p-q}\in\Big(0,\tfrac{N-p}{p}\Big),
\qquad
c:=\big[\kappa^{p-1}\big(N-p-(p-1)\kappa\big)\big]^{-\frac{1}{p-q}} .
\]
Then $u(x)=c|x|^{-\kappa}$ belongs to $W^{1,p}(B_{1})\cap L^{p^{*}}(B_{1})$, is unbounded near the origin, and solves
\[
-\Delta_{p}u=\omega(x)u^{q-1}\quad\text{in }B_{1},
\qquad \omega(x)=|x|^{-a}\in L^{\frac{2N}{\sigma}}(B_{1}).
\]
The interval $(p,\sigma/2)$ is nonempty precisely when $\sigma>2p$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Let $a\in(p,\sigma/2)$, so that $\kappa=(a-p)/(p-q)>0$ because $q<p$ and $a>p$. From $p-q=p(\sigma-2p)/(2(N-p))$ we get
\[
\kappa<\frac{N-p}{p}\iff a-p<\frac{(N-p)(p-q)}{p}=\frac{\sigma-2p}{2}\iff a<\frac{\sigma}{2},
\]
so $\kappa\in(0,\gamma_{e})$. In particular $\kappa<\gamma_{h}$, so $h(\kappa)=\kappa^{p-1}(N-p-(p-1)\kappa)>0$ and, by \eqref{eq:barrier},
\[
-\Delta_{p}\big(c|x|^{-\kappa}\big)=h(\kappa)c^{p-1}|x|^{-\kappa(p-1)-p} .
\]
Since $\kappa(p-q)=a-p$, we have $\kappa(p-1)+p=a+(q-1)\kappa$, so the right hand side equals $h(\kappa)c^{p-q}\cdot|x|^{-a}\big(c|x|^{-\kappa}\big)^{q-1}$, and the stated choice of $c$ makes $h(\kappa)c^{p-q}=1$. The singularity is not a Dirac mass because $\kappa<\gamma_{h}$, and the right hand side is in $L^{1}(B_{1})$ because $\kappa(p-1)+p<N$, so $u$ is a weak solution in $B_{1}$. Finally $\int_{B_{1}}|\nabla u|^{p}\simeq\int_{0}^{1}r^{N-1-(\kappa+1)p}dr<\infty$ and $\int_{B_{1}}u^{p^{*}}<\infty$, both because $\kappa<\gamma_{e}$, while $|x|^{-a}\in L^{2N/\sigma}(B_{1})$ because $2Na/\sigma<N$ is equivalent to $a<\sigma/2$. The interval $(p,\sigma/2)$ is nonempty if and only if $\sigma>2p$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:lb-admissible}
Let $u\in D^{1,p}(\mathbb R^N)$ be a nonnegative weak solution of \eqref{eq:main}, with $q>1$, and put $f=W[u]u^{q-1}$. Then
\[
W[u]\in L^{2N/\sigma}(\mathbb R^N),
\qquad
f\in L^{(p^*)'}(\mathbb R^N).
\]
Consequently the weak formulation \eqref{eq:weak} extends by density to every compactly supported $\varphi\in L^{p^*}$ with $\nabla\varphi\in L^p$.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
The Hardy--Littlewood--Sobolev inequality applied to $u^q$, with $p^*/q=2N/(2N-\sigma)$, gives
\[
\|W[u]\|_{L^{2N/\sigma}}\le C\|u\|_{L^{p^*}}^q.
\]
Moreover
\[
\frac{\sigma}{2N}+\frac{q-1}{p^*}=1-\frac1{p^*},
\]
so H\"older's inequality gives $f\in L^{(p^*)'}$. The extension of the test class follows from density in $D^{1,p}$ on compact subsets.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:lb-far}
For every $x_0\in\mathbb R^N$, $\rho>0$ and $x\in B_{\rho/2}(x_0)$,
\[
\int_{|y-x_0|\ge\rho}\frac{u^q(y)}{|x-y|^\sigma}\,dy
\le C\|u\|_{L^{p^*}(\mathbb R^N)}^q\rho^{-\sigma/2}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
For such $x,y$ one has $|x-y|\ge |y-x_0|/2$. H\"older's inequality with the conjugate exponents $p^*/q$ and $2N/\sigma$ yields
\[
\int_{|y-x_0|\ge\rho}\frac{u^q(y)}{|x-y|^\sigma}\,dy
\le C\|u\|_{L^{p^*}}^q
\left(\int_{|z|\ge\rho}|z|^{-2N}\,dz\right)^{\sigma/(2N)},
\]
which is the asserted estimate.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:lb-hole}
There are $\theta_0\in(0,1)$ and $C>0$, depending only on $N,p$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lb-hole}
\mathcal D(r)\le\theta_0\mathcal D(2r)+C\,T(r),
\end{equation}
where $A_r=B_{2r}(x_0)\setminus B_r(x_0)$, $\bar u_{A_r}$ denotes the mean of $u$ over $A_r$, and
\[
T(r):=\int_{B_{2r}(x_0)}W[u]u^{q-1}(u-\bar u_{A_r})^+.
\]
Moreover
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lb-SP}
\|(u-\bar u_{A_r})^+\|_{L^{p^*}(B_{2r}(x_0))}
\le C\mathcal D(2r)^{1/p}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Choose $\eta\in C_c^\infty(B_{2r}(x_0))$ with $0\le\eta\le1$, $\eta=1$ on $B_r(x_0)$ and $|\nabla\eta|\le2/r$. By Lemma \ref{lem:lb-admissible}, $(u-\bar u_{A_r})\eta^p$ is an admissible test function. Since the right-hand side of the equation is nonnegative,
\[
\int\eta^p|\nabla u|^p
+p\int\eta^{p-1}(u-\bar u_{A_r})|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\eta
\le T(r).
\]
Young's inequality and the scale-invariant Poincar\'e inequality on $A_r$ give
\[
\mathcal D(r)\le C_2\big(\mathcal D(2r)-\mathcal D(r)\big)+2T(r),
\]
which yields \eqref{eq:lb-hole} after rearrangement.  To prove \eqref{eq:lb-SP}, let $\bar u_{B_{2r}}$ be the mean on $B_{2r}(x_0)$. The Sobolev--Poincar\'e inequality gives
\[
\|u-\bar u_{B_{2r}}\|_{L^{p^*}(B_{2r})}\le C\mathcal D(2r)^{1/p},
\]
and H\"older's inequality controls $|\bar u_{A_r}-\bar u_{B_{2r}}||B_{2r}|^{1/p^*}$ by the same quantity. This proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:lb-hartree}
Fix $\rho_0>0$. For $0<r\le\rho_0/4$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lb-TB}
T(r)\le C\mathcal B(r)\mathcal D(2r)^{1/p},
\end{equation}
where, with $C_u:=\|u\|_{L^{p^*}(\mathbb R^N)}^q$,
\begin{align*}
\mathcal B(r):={}&\mathcal Z(4r)^{2q-1}
+\left(\sum_{\substack{j\ge1\\2^{j+1}r<\rho_0}}
2^{-j\sigma/2}\mathcal Z(2^{j+2}r)^q\right)\mathcal Z(2r)^{q-1}\\
&+C_u\left(\frac r{\rho_0}\right)^{\sigma/2}\mathcal Z(2r)^{q-1}.
\end{align*}
Consequently, for every $\varepsilon>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lb-TY}
T(r)\le\varepsilon\mathcal D(2r)+C_\varepsilon\mathcal B(r)^{p'}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Split $W=W_1+W_2+W_3$ according to the regions $B_{4r}(x_0)$, $B_{\rho_0}(x_0)\setminus B_{4r}(x_0)$ and $\mathbb R^N\setminus B_{\rho_0}(x_0)$, and let $T_i$ denote the corresponding parts of $T(r)$. Put
\[
g:=u^{q-1}(u-\bar u_{A_r})^+\chi_{B_{2r}(x_0)}.
\]
For the near part, HLS and $qt'=p^*$ give
\[
T_1\le C\|g\|_{L^{t'}}\|u^q\|_{L^{t'}(B_{4r})}
\le C\mathcal Z(4r)^{2q-1}\mathcal D(2r)^{1/p},
\]
where we used \eqref{eq:lb-SP} and $q>1$.

We shall also use
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lb-common}
\int_{B_{2r}}u^{q-1}(u-\bar u_{A_r})^+
\le C\mathcal Z(2r)^{q-1}\mathcal D(2r)^{1/p}r^{\sigma/2},
\end{equation}
which follows from H\"older's inequality, \eqref{eq:lb-SP}, and
$N(1-q/p^*)=\sigma/2$.

For the middle part, cover $B_{\rho_0}(x_0)\setminus B_{4r}(x_0)$ by the dyadic annuli
\[
D_j=\{2^{j+1}r\le |y-x_0|<2^{j+2}r\},
\qquad j\ge1,
\]
with $2^{j+1}r<\rho_0$. For $x\in B_{2r}(x_0)$, H\"older's inequality gives
\[
\int_{D_j}\frac{u^q(y)}{|x-y|^\sigma}\,dy
\le C(2^jr)^{-\sigma/2}\mathcal Z(2^{j+2}r)^q.
\]
Combining this with \eqref{eq:lb-common} gives the second term in \eqref{eq:lb-TB}. The far part follows from Lemma \ref{lem:lb-far} and \eqref{eq:lb-common}. Summing the three contributions proves \eqref{eq:lb-TB}; Young's inequality gives \eqref{eq:lb-TY}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:lb-chain}
Let $r_k=2^{-k}\rho_0$, $e_k=\mathcal D(r_k)$ and $z_k=\mathcal Z(r_k)$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lb-chain}
z_k\le C\left(\sum_{i=0}^k2^{-(k-i)\gamma_e}e_i^{1/p}
+2^{-k\gamma_e}z_0\right).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Let $\bar u_k$ be the mean of $u$ on $B_{r_k}(x_0)$. Sobolev--Poincar\'e gives
\[
z_k\le Ce_k^{1/p}+C|\bar u_k|r_k^{\gamma_e}.
\]
Moreover
\[
|\bar u_{i+1}-\bar u_i|\le Cr_i^{-\gamma_e}e_i^{1/p},
\qquad
|\bar u_0|\le C\rho_0^{-\gamma_e}z_0.
\]
Telescoping the means and using $r_k/r_i=2^{-(k-i)}$ yields \eqref{eq:lb-chain}.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:lb-discrete}
Let $(e_k)$ be nonnegative, $c_*>0$, and
\[
\widetilde E_k:=\max_{0\le i\le k}2^{-c_*(k-i)}e_i.
\]
If for some $\theta\in(0,1)$, $\beta>0$ and $A\ge0$,
\[
e_{k+1}\le\theta\widetilde E_k+A2^{-k\beta}\qquad(k\ge1),
\]
then there is $\alpha>0$ and $C>0$ such that $e_k\le\widetilde E_k\le C2^{-k\alpha}$.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Since
\[
\widetilde E_{k+1}
=\max\{e_{k+1},2^{-c_*}\widetilde E_k\}
\le\theta_2\widetilde E_k+A2^{-k\beta},
\qquad
\theta_2:=\max\{\theta,2^{-c_*}\}<1,
\]
iteration gives
\[
\widetilde E_k\le (\widetilde E_1+Ak)\Lambda^{k-1},
\qquad
\Lambda:=\max\{\theta_2,2^{-\beta}\}<1.
\]
Any $0<\alpha<-\log_2\Lambda$ gives the conclusion.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:lb-morrey}
For every compact $K\subset\mathbb R^N$ there are
\[
\alpha\in(0,N-p),\qquad \rho_0\in(0,1],\qquad C>0,
\]
where $\alpha$ may be chosen depending only on $N,p,\sigma$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lb-morrey}
\int_{B_r(x_0)}|\nabla u|^p\le Cr^\alpha
\end{equation}
for every $x_0\in K$ and $0<r\le\rho_0$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
By the uniform absolute continuity of the integrals of $|\nabla u|^p$ and $u^{p^*}$, for every $\varepsilon_0>0$ one may choose $\rho_0\in(0,1]$, uniformly for $x_0\in K$, so that
\[
\mathcal D(\rho_0)\le\varepsilon_0^p,
\qquad
\mathcal Z(\rho_0)\le\varepsilon_0.
\]
Set
\[
c_*:=\frac12\min\left\{p\gamma_e,\frac{p\sigma}{2q}\right\},
\qquad
\beta_1:=\min\left\{\gamma_e,\frac\sigma{2q}\right\},
\qquad
P_k:=2^{-k\beta_1},
\]
and define $\widetilde E_k$ as in Lemma \ref{lem:lb-discrete}. Since $e_i\le2^{c_*(k-i)}\widetilde E_k$, Lemma \ref{lem:lb-chain} and $c_*/p<\gamma_e$ give, for $0\le m\le k$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lb-zm}
z_m\le C\left(2^{c_*(k-m)/p}\widetilde E_k^{1/p}+2^{-m\gamma_e}\right).
\end{equation}

Apply Lemma \ref{lem:lb-hartree} with $r=r_{k+1}$. Then $2r=r_k$, $4r=r_{k-1}$, and the dyadic sum has $1\le j\le k-1$. From \eqref{eq:lb-zm},
\[
z_k+z_{k-1}\le C\big(\widetilde E_k^{1/p}+P_k\big).
\]
For the middle sum, \eqref{eq:lb-zm} with $m=k-1-j$ gives
\begin{align*}
\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}2^{-j\sigma/2}z_{k-1-j}^q
&\le C\widetilde E_k^{q/p}
\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j(\sigma/2-c_*q/p)}\\
&\quad+C\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}
2^{-j\sigma/2-(k-1-j)q\gamma_e}.
\end{align*}
The first series converges because $c_*q/p<\sigma/2$. Since $q\gamma_e-\sigma/2>0$, the second is a geometric sum dominated by its last term and is bounded by $C2^{-k\sigma/2}$. By the definition of $\beta_1$, $2^{-k\sigma/2}\le P_k^q$. Therefore
\[
\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}2^{-j\sigma/2}z_{k-1-j}^q
\le C\big(\widetilde E_k^{1/p}+P_k\big)^q.
\]
The final term in $\mathcal B(r_{k+1})$ is bounded by $C_u2^{-k\sigma/2}$ because $z_k\le z_0\le\varepsilon_0\le1$. Hence
\[
\mathcal B(r_{k+1})
\le C\big(\widetilde E_k^{1/p}+P_k\big)^{2q-1}
+C_u2^{-k\sigma/2}.
\]
Raising this inequality to $p'$ and separating the pure dyadic powers gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lb-Bpower}
\mathcal B(r_{k+1})^{p'}
\le C\widetilde E_k^{(2q-1)/(p-1)}+C2^{-k\beta_2}
\end{equation}
for some $\beta_2>0$, where the last constant may depend on $u$. By \eqref{eq:lb-identities2},
\[
\widetilde E_k^{(2q-1)/(p-1)}
=\widetilde E_k^{(2q-p)/(p-1)}\widetilde E_k
\le\varepsilon_0^{p(2q-p)/(p-1)}\widetilde E_k.
\]
Combining \eqref{eq:lb-hole}, \eqref{eq:lb-TY} and \eqref{eq:lb-Bpower}, first choose the Young parameter so that the coefficient inherited from \eqref{eq:lb-hole} remains $<1$, and then choose $\varepsilon_0$ so small that the superlinear term above is absorbed. We obtain
\[
e_{k+1}\le\theta\widetilde E_k+A2^{-k\beta}
\]
with $\theta\in(0,1)$ and $\beta>0$, uniformly in $x_0\in K$. Lemma \ref{lem:lb-discrete} therefore yields $e_k\le C2^{-k\alpha}$ for some $\alpha>0$. Decreasing $\alpha$ if necessary, we may assume $\alpha<N-p$. Interpolation between consecutive dyadic radii gives \eqref{eq:lb-morrey}.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:lb-first-gain}
There exists $\widetilde p>p^*$ such that
\[
u\in L^{\widetilde p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^N).
\]
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Fix $z\in\mathbb R^N$ and apply Proposition \ref{prop:lb-morrey} with $K=\overline{B_2(z)}$. Let $B=B_{\rho_0}(z)$ and put $g=|\nabla u|\chi_B$. We verify the global Morrey bound needed below. If $B_r(y)\cap B=\emptyset$, there is nothing to prove. If $B_r(y)\cap B\ne\emptyset$ and $r<\rho_0$, then $|y-z|<r+\rho_0<2$, hence $y\in K$, and Proposition \ref{prop:lb-morrey} gives
\[
\int_{B_r(y)}g^p\le Cr^\alpha.
\]
If $r\ge\rho_0$, then
\[
\int_{B_r(y)}g^p\le\|\nabla u\|_{L^p(\mathbb R^N)}^p
\le\|\nabla u\|_{L^p}^p\rho_0^{-\alpha}r^\alpha.
\]
Thus
\[
\sup_{y\in\mathbb R^N,\,r>0}r^{-\alpha}\int_{B_r(y)}g^p<\infty,
\]
so $g$ belongs to the Morrey class $M^{p,\alpha}$. The pointwise potential estimate \cite[Lemma 7.16]{GilbargTrudinger2001} gives, for a.e. $x\in B$,
\[
|u(x)-\bar u_B|
\le C\int_B\frac{|\nabla u(y)|}{|x-y|^{N-1}}\,dy
\le C I_1g(x).
\]
Adams' theorem \cite{Adams1975}, in the Morrey normalization
\[
\|g\|_{M^{p,\alpha}}^p
=\sup_{y,r}r^{-\alpha}\int_{B_r(y)}|g|^p,
\]
states that, for $0<\alpha<N-p$,
\[
I_1:M^{p,\alpha}\longrightarrow M^{\widetilde p,\alpha},
\qquad
\frac1{\widetilde p}=\frac1p-\frac1{N-\alpha}.
\]
Since $\alpha>0$,
\[
\frac1{\widetilde p}<\frac1p-\frac1N=\frac1{p^*},
\]
and hence $\widetilde p>p^*$. Thus $u\in L^{\widetilde p}(B)$, and a finite covering proves the local assertion.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:lb-moser}
Assume $u\in L^R_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^N)$ for some $R>p^*$ with $R\ne r_H$. Then $u\in L^{R'}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^N)$, where
\[
R'-p^*\ge\mu(R-p^*)\quad\text{if }R<r_H,
\qquad
R'=\frac{p^*}{p}R\quad\text{if }R>r_H.
\]
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Fix a ball $B_\rho=B_\rho(x_1)$ and split $W=W_1+W_2$, where $W_1$ is the convolution restricted to $B_{2\rho}(x_1)$. Lemma \ref{lem:lb-far} gives $W_2\in L^\infty(B_\rho)$. If $R<r_H$, HLS gives
\[
W_1\in L^t(B_\rho),
\qquad
\frac1t=\frac qR-\frac{N-\sigma}{N}>0.
\]
If $R>r_H$, H\"older's inequality gives $W_1\in L^\infty(B_\rho)$; in this case set $1/t=0$.

Let $s>1$, $k>0$, $u_k=\min\{u,k\}$, and $\beta=(s-1)p$. To avoid any chain-rule issue at the zero set when $0<\beta<1$, define
\[
F_{k,\beta}(t):=t\min\{t,k\}^{\beta},\qquad t\ge0.
\]
This function is globally Lipschitz on $[0,\infty)$ and satisfies
\[
F_{k,\beta}'(t)
=\min\{t,k\}^{\beta}
+\beta t\min\{t,k\}^{\beta-1}\chi_{\{t<k\}}
\quad\text{for a.e. }t>0.
\]
Choose $\eta\in C_c^\infty(B_\rho)$ with $0\le\eta\le1$ and $\eta=1$ on $B_{\rho/2}$. The test function $\varphi=\eta^pF_{k,\beta}(u)$ is admissible by Lemma \ref{lem:lb-admissible}. Testing and using Young's inequality gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lb-moser1}
\int\eta^pu_k^\beta|\nabla u|^p
+\beta\int\eta^pu_k^\beta|\nabla u_k|^p
\le C\int|\nabla\eta|^pu^pu_k^\beta
+2\int\eta^pWu^qu_k^\beta.
\end{equation}
Define likewise $G_{k,s}(t)=t\min\{t,k\}^{s-1}$ and $w_k=G_{k,s}(u)$. Then $G_{k,s}$ is globally Lipschitz and
\[
|\nabla w_k|
\le u_k^{s-1}|\nabla u|+(s-1)u_k^{s-1}|\nabla u_k|.
\]
Using \eqref{eq:lb-moser1} and the Sobolev inequality for $\eta w_k$ yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lb-moser2}
\left(\int(\eta w_k)^{p^*}\right)^{p/p^*}
\le Cs^{p-1}\left(
\int_{B_\rho}|\nabla\eta|^pu^{sp}
+\int_{B_\rho}\eta^pWu^{q+(s-1)p}
\right).
\end{equation}
The right-hand side is finite, uniformly in $k$, provided
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lb-moser-cond}
sp\le R,
\qquad
\frac1t+\frac{q+(s-1)p}{R}\le1.
\end{equation}
Then monotone convergence gives $u\in L^{sp^*}(B_{\rho/2})$.

If $R<r_H$, the second condition in \eqref{eq:lb-moser-cond} becomes
\[
(s-1)p\le\frac{2N-\sigma}{N}(R-p^*).
\]
Choose
\[
s=1+\min\left\{
\frac{2N-\sigma}{Np}(R-p^*),\frac Rp-1
\right\}.
\]
Then
\[
sp^*=\min\left\{
p^*+\frac{2q}{p}(R-p^*),\frac{p^*}{p}R
\right\},
\]
so $sp^*-p^*\ge\mu(R-p^*)$. If $R>r_H$, take $s=R/p$. Since $q<p$ by $\sigma>2p$, the second condition in \eqref{eq:lb-moser-cond} holds, and $sp^*=(p^*/p)R$. This proves the lemma.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
By Proposition \ref{prop:lb-first-gain}, $u\in L^{\widetilde p}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for some $\widetilde p>p^*$. Choose $R_0\in(p^*,\widetilde p]$ with $R_0\ne r_H$ and set
\[
\mu_1:=\frac{1+\mu}{2}>1.
\]
Iterate Lemma \ref{lem:lb-moser}. If at a step with $R_j<r_H$ the exponent furnished by the lemma equals $r_H$, then $u\in L^{r_H}_{\mathrm{loc}}$, hence also $u\in L^{r_H-\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for every sufficiently small $\delta>0$. Because
\[
r_H-p^*\ge\mu(R_j-p^*)>\mu_1(R_j-p^*),
\]
we may choose $\delta$ so small that
\[
r_H-\delta-p^*\ge\mu_1(R_j-p^*)
\]
and continue from $R_{j+1}=r_H-\delta$. Thus the endpoint $r_H$ causes no loss of a uniform expansion factor. While $R_j<r_H$, the quantity $R_j-p^*$ therefore grows at least geometrically with ratio $\mu_1>1$, so after finitely many steps one obtains an exponent $R_j>r_H$. Thereafter Lemma \ref{lem:lb-moser} gives
\[
R_{j+1}=\frac{p^*}{p}R_j,
\]
and hence $R_j\to\infty$. Therefore
\[
u\in L^s_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^N)
\qquad\text{for every }s<\infty.
\]

Fix a ball $B_\rho$. Taking $R>r_H$ in the first part of the proof of Lemma \ref{lem:lb-moser} gives $W\in L^\infty(B_\rho)$. Since $q>1$, it follows that $Wu^{q-1}\in L^m(B_\rho)$ for every finite $m$. In particular choose $m>N/p$. Serrin's local boundedness theorem for quasilinear equations \cite{Serrin1964} then yields
\[
u\in L^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^N),
\]
as claimed.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:powergain}
Let $N\ge3$, $p>1$ and $2p<\sigma<N$, and assume that $q:=p^*_{\sigma}>1$. Let $u$ be a nonnegative $D^{1,p}(\mathbb R^N)$-weak solution of \eqref{eq:main}. Then there are $C>0$ and $\tau>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:automatic-power}
u(x)\le C|x|^{-\gamma_{e}-\tau}\qquad\text{for }|x|\ge1.
\end{equation}
In fact,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-vanish-infinity}
|x|^{\gamma_{e}}u(x)\longrightarrow0\qquad\text{as }|x|\to\infty.
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:critical-infinity}
Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:powergain}, either $u\equiv0$, or $u>0$ in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-bound-infinity}
\sup_{|x|\ge1}|x|^{\gamma_{e}}u(x)<\infty,
\end{equation}
and \eqref{eq:critical-vanish-infinity} holds.
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
By Corollary \ref{cor:structure}, $u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$ and $u(x)\to0$ as $|x|\to\infty$. Since $-\Delta_{p}u\ge0$, the weak Harnack inequality implies that either $u\equiv0$ or $u>0$ everywhere. We assume the latter and put $M:=u^{1/\gamma_{e}}$.

Suppose first that $\sup_{x\ne0}M(x)|x|=\infty$. Fix $k\in\mathbb{N}$ and choose $y_{k}\ne0$ with $M(y_{k})|y_{k}|>2k$. Apply Lemma \ref{lem:doubling} with $X=\Sigma=\mathbb{R}^{N}$, $D=\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus\{0\}$ and $\Gamma=\{0\}$. We obtain $x_{k}\in D$ such that
\[
M(x_{k})|x_{k}|>2k,
\qquad
M(z)\le2M(x_{k})\quad\text{if }|z-x_{k}|\le\frac{k}{M(x_{k})}.
\]
Let $\lambda_{k}:=M(x_{k})^{-1}$ and
\[
v_{k}(z):=\lambda_{k}^{\gamma_{e}}u(x_{k}+\lambda_{k}z).
\]
Then $v_{k}$ solves \eqref{eq:main}, $v_{k}(0)=1$, and $0<v_{k}\le2^{\gamma_{e}}$ on $B_{k}$. Since $u$ is globally bounded, $|x_{k}|\to\infty$, while
\[
\frac{\lambda_{k}}{|x_{k}|}<\frac1{2k}.
\]
For every fixed $R>0$ and all large $k$, Lemma \ref{lem:Wloc}, the scaling identity \eqref{eq:scaleinv}, and the local regularity estimates of \cite{DiBenedetto1983,Tolksdorf1984} give a uniform $C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{B_{R}})$ bound for $v_{k}$. On the other hand,
\[
\int_{B_{R}}v_{k}^{p^{*}}dz
=\int_{B_{R\lambda_{k}}(x_{k})}u^{p^{*}}dx\longrightarrow0,
\]
because
\[
B_{R\lambda_{k}}(x_{k})\subset
\left\{|x|\ge |x_{k}|\left(1-\frac{R}{2k}\right)\right\}
\]
and the right-hand side escapes to infinity. A subsequence therefore converges locally uniformly to a continuous function which is identically zero, contradicting $v_{k}(0)=1$. This proves \eqref{eq:critical-bound-infinity}.

We next prove \eqref{eq:critical-vanish-infinity}. If it fails, there are $\delta>0$ and $y_{j}$ with $r_{j}:=|y_{j}|\to\infty$ such that $r_{j}^{\gamma_{e}}u(y_{j})\ge\delta$. Put $\lambda_{j}:=r_{j}/2$ and
\[
v_{j}(z):=\lambda_{j}^{\gamma_{e}}u(y_{j}+\lambda_{j}z).
\]
For $|z|\le3/2$ one has $|y_{j}+\lambda_{j}z|\ge r_{j}/4$, so \eqref{eq:critical-bound-infinity} gives a uniform $L^{\infty}(B_{3/2})$ bound for $v_{j}$. Lemma \ref{lem:Wloc} and the local regularity theory then give a uniform $C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{B_{1/2}})$ bound, whereas
\[
\int_{B_{1}}v_{j}^{p^{*}}dz
=\int_{B_{\lambda_{j}}(y_{j})}u^{p^{*}}dx\longrightarrow0
\]
because $B_{\lambda_{j}}(y_{j})\subset\{|x|\ge r_{j}/2\}$. Hence $v_{j}\to0$ uniformly on $B_{1/2}$ along a subsequence, while
\[
v_{j}(0)=2^{-\gamma_{e}}r_{j}^{\gamma_{e}}u(y_{j})\ge2^{-\gamma_{e}}\delta,
\]
a contradiction.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
By Theorem \ref{thm:local-boundedness} and Corollary \ref{cor:structure}, $u\in L^\infty(\mathbb R^N)\cap C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb R^N)$ and $u(x)\to0$ as $|x|\to\infty$. If $u\equiv0$ there is nothing to prove, so by Lemma \ref{lem:critical-infinity} we may assume $u>0$. For $r\ge1$ set
\[
A(r):=r^{\gamma_{e}}\max_{r\le|x|\le2r}u(x).
\]
By Lemma \ref{lem:critical-infinity}, $A$ is bounded and $A(r)\to0$ as $r\to\infty$.

Suppose, for contradiction, that \eqref{eq:automatic-power} fails for every $\tau>0$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:no-power}
\sup_{r\ge1}r^{\tau}A(r)=\infty\qquad\text{for every }\tau>0.
\end{equation}
Choose
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eps-choice}
0<\varepsilon<\min\left\{\gamma_{e},\ \gamma_{h}-\gamma_{e},\ \frac{\sigma}{2q}\right\}.
\end{equation}
Write $a(t):=A(e^{t})$ for $t\ge0$ and define the weighted envelope
\[
E(t):=\sup_{s\ge0}a(s)e^{-\varepsilon|s-t|}.
\]
Since $a$ is bounded and tends to zero, $E(t)\to0$ as $t\to\infty$. By \eqref{eq:no-power} with $\tau=\varepsilon/2$, we can choose $t_{k}\to\infty$ so that
\[
e^{\varepsilon t_{k}/2}a(t_{k})\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
Choose $s_{k}\ge0$ such that
\[
a(s_{k})e^{-\varepsilon|s_{k}-t_{k}|}\ge\frac12E(t_{k}).
\]
Then $s_{k}\to\infty$. Indeed, a bounded subsequence of $(s_{k})$ would give $a(t_{k})\le C e^{-\varepsilon t_{k}}$, contradicting the preceding display. Put
\[
r_{k}:=e^{s_{k}},\qquad A_{k}:=a(s_{k})=A(r_{k}).
\]
Then $A_{k}\to0$, and for every $s\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:envelope-ratio}
\frac{a(s)}{A_{k}}
\le2e^{\varepsilon|s-s_{k}|}.
\end{equation}
Taking $s=0$ also gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Ak-lower}
A_{k}\ge\frac{a(0)}{2}r_{k}^{-\varepsilon}.
\end{equation}

Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wk-blowdown}
w_{k}(x):=\frac{r_{k}^{\gamma_{e}}}{A_{k}}u(r_{k}x).
\end{equation}
Since $r_{k}^{\gamma_{e}}u(r_{k}\cdot)$ solves \eqref{eq:main}, homogeneity gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wk-equation}
-\Delta_{p}w_{k}
=A_{k}^{2q-p}\big(|x|^{-\sigma}*w_{k}^{q}\big)w_{k}^{q-1}.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wk-normalization}
\max_{1\le|x|\le2}w_{k}(x)=1.
\end{equation}
If $r_{k}|x|\ge1$, then \eqref{eq:envelope-ratio} yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wk-growth}
w_{k}(x)
\le2|x|^{-\gamma_{e}}e^{\varepsilon|\log|x||}
\le
\begin{cases}
2|x|^{-\gamma_{e}-\varepsilon},&0<|x|\le1,\\
2|x|^{-\gamma_{e}+\varepsilon},&|x|\ge1.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}

We claim that $W[w_{k}]:=|x|^{-\sigma}*w_{k}^{q}$ is uniformly bounded on every compact annulus. Fix $0<\rho<R<\infty$ and $x$ with $\rho\le|x|\le R$. The contribution of $|y|<r_{k}^{-1}$ is bounded, using Theorem \ref{thm:local-boundedness}, by
\[
C\int_{|y|<r_{k}^{-1}}w_{k}^{q}dy
=C A_{k}^{-q}r_{k}^{q\gamma_{e}-N}\int_{B_{1}}u^{q}dz
=C A_{k}^{-q}r_{k}^{-\sigma/2}
\le C r_{k}^{q\varepsilon-\sigma/2},
\]
which is uniformly bounded, indeed tends to zero, by \eqref{eq:eps-choice} and \eqref{eq:Ak-lower}. On the region $r_{k}^{-1}\le|y|\le\rho/2$, \eqref{eq:wk-growth} is integrable because
\[
q(\gamma_{e}+\varepsilon)<q\gamma_{e}+\frac{\sigma}{2}=N.
\]
On a fixed annulus containing $x$, \eqref{eq:wk-growth} gives a uniform bound for $w_{k}$ and the kernel is locally integrable because $\sigma<N$. Finally, for large $|y|$,
\[
|x-y|^{-\sigma}w_{k}^{q}(y)
\le C|y|^{-\sigma-q(\gamma_{e}-\varepsilon)},
\]
and this is integrable at infinity because
\[
\sigma+q(\gamma_{e}-\varepsilon)>N.
\]
The claim follows.

Since $2q-p>0$ by Lemma \ref{lem:exponents}(a), the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:wk-equation} tends to zero uniformly on compact annuli. The local regularity theory therefore yields, after passing to a subsequence,
\[
w_{k}\longrightarrow w\qquad\text{in }C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus\{0\}),
\]
where $w\ge0$ is $p$-harmonic in $\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus\{0\}$. Choose $z_{k}$ with $1\le|z_{k}|\le2$ and $w_{k}(z_{k})=1$. Passing to a further subsequence, $z_{k}\to z_{\infty}$, and local uniform convergence gives $w(z_{\infty})=1$. Thus $w\not\equiv0$. Passing to the limit in \eqref{eq:wk-growth},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:w-limit-growth}
w(x)\le
\begin{cases}
2|x|^{-\gamma_{e}-\varepsilon},&0<|x|\le1,\\
2|x|^{-\gamma_{e}+\varepsilon},&|x|\ge1.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Because $\gamma_{e}+\varepsilon<\gamma_{h}$, Serrin's classification of isolated singularities for nonnegative $p$-harmonic functions \cite{Serrin1965} implies that the origin is removable: a nonremovable singularity is comparable to the fundamental $p$-harmonic profile $|x|^{-\gamma_{h}}$. Thus $w$ extends to a nonnegative entire $p$-harmonic function. Since $\varepsilon<\gamma_{e}$, \eqref{eq:w-limit-growth} gives $w(x)\to0$ as $|x|\to\infty$. The maximum principle on expanding balls then yields $w\equiv0$, contradicting \eqref{eq:wk-normalization}. Hence \eqref{eq:automatic-power} holds for some $\tau>0$.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:bootstrap}
Let $N\ge3$, $p>1$ and $2p<\sigma<N$, and assume that $q:=p^*_{\sigma}>1$ (this is automatic when $\Lambda>0$ by Lemma \ref{lem:qbigger1}). Let $u$ be a positive $D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$-weak solution of \eqref{eq:main}. Then for every $\nu<\min\{\gamma_{h},\gamma_{*}\}$ there is $C_{\nu}>0$ with
\[
u(x)\le C_{\nu}|x|^{-\nu}
\qquad\text{for }|x|\ge1 .
\]
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:Wbounded}
If in addition $u\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$, then $W\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$ with
\[
\|W\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\le C\big(\|u\|^{q}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}+\|u\|^{q}_{L^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\big).
\]
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Apply Lemma \ref{lem:Wloc} with $r=1$ and an arbitrary center.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:regularity}
We have $u\in C^{1,\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$ for some $0<\alpha<\min\{1,\frac{1}{p-1}\}$. Moreover either $u\equiv0$ or $u>0$ in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$, and in the latter case $\inf_{K}u>0$ for every compact $K\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}$.
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
By Lemma \ref{lem:Wbounded} and $q>1$, the right hand side of \eqref{eq:main} satisfies $Wu^{q-1}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$. Hence $u$ is a bounded weak solution of $-\Delta_{p}u=f$ with $f\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}$, and the regularity results of \cite{DiBenedetto1983,Tolksdorf1984} give $u\in C^{1,\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}$.

Since $Wu^{q-1}\ge0$, the function $u$ is $p$-superharmonic. By the weak Harnack inequality \cite{Trudinger1967}, for every ball $B_{2r}(x_{0})$ and every $0<t<N(p-1)/(N-p)$,
\[
\inf_{B_{r}(x_{0})}u\ge c\Big(\frac{1}{|B_{2r}|}\int_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}u^{t}\,dy\Big)^{1/t},
\]
so the set where $u$ vanishes on a ball is open and closed, whence $u\equiv0$ or $u>0$. In the latter case the same inequality, together with continuity and compactness, gives $\inf_{K}u>0$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem:Tprops}
Let $N\ge3$, $p>1$ and $2p<\sigma<N$. Then $a$ and $T$ are nondecreasing and continuous on $(\gamma_{e},\infty)$, and
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item $T(\gamma_{e})=\gamma_{e}$;
\item on the set where $q\delta+\sigma-N<\sigma$, the map $T$ is affine with slope $(2q-1)/(p-1)>1$;
\item on the set where $q\delta+\sigma-N\ge\sigma$, the map $T$ is affine with slope $(q-1)/(p-1)<1$ and unique fixed point $\gamma_{*}$;
\item $q\gamma_{*}>N$, so $\gamma_{*}$ lies in the saturated branch, and $\gamma_{e}$ and $\gamma_{*}$ are the only fixed points of $T$ in $(0,\infty)$;
\item $T$ is strictly increasing, $T(\delta)>\gamma_{e}$ for every $\delta>\gamma_{e}$, and $T(\delta)>\delta$ for every $\delta\in(\gamma_{e},\gamma_{*})$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
The map $a$ is nondecreasing and continuous, and $\delta\mapsto(q-1)\delta$ is strictly increasing because $q>1$, so $T$ is strictly increasing and continuous. For (a), $q\gamma_{e}=N-\sigma/2<N$, so $a(\gamma_{e})=q\gamma_{e}+\sigma-N=\sigma/2$, and
\[
T(\gamma_{e})=\frac{\sigma/2+(q-1)\gamma_{e}-p}{p-1}
=\frac{\sigma/2+N-\sigma/2-\gamma_{e}-p}{p-1}
=\frac{(p-1)(N-p)}{p(p-1)}=\gamma_{e},
\]
where we used $q\gamma_{e}=N-\sigma/2$. Claims (b) and (c) are immediate from \eqref{eq:adelta}, together with $2q>p$ and $q<p$ from Lemma \ref{lem:exponents}(a), and the computation of the fixed points of the two affine branches: in case (b) it is $(N+p-\sigma)/(2q-p)=\gamma_{e}$, and in case (c) it is $(\sigma-p)/(p-q)=\gamma_{*}$. For (d), a computation gives
\[
q\gamma_{*}-N=\frac{\sigma(N+p-\sigma)}{\sigma-2p}>0
\]
because $\sigma<N$ and $\sigma>2p$, so $\gamma_{*}$ indeed belongs to the saturated branch, while $q\gamma_{e}=N-\sigma/2<N$ places $\gamma_{e}$ in the unsaturated one. Since each branch is affine with a single fixed point, these are the only two. For (e), strict monotonicity was observed above, and $T(\delta)>T(\gamma_{e})=\gamma_{e}$ for $\delta>\gamma_{e}$. On the unsaturated branch $T$ is affine with slope larger than $1$ and fixed point $\gamma_{e}$, so $T(\delta)>\delta$ there for $\delta>\gamma_{e}$. On the saturated branch $T$ is affine with slope smaller than $1$ and fixed point $\gamma_{*}$, so $T(\delta)>\delta$ there exactly for $\delta<\gamma_{*}$.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lem:onestep}
Assume the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:bootstrap}, let $\delta>\gamma_{e}$ and suppose that $u(x)\le C_{\delta}|x|^{-\delta}$ for $|x|\ge1$. Then for every
\[
\nu<\min\{\gamma_{h},\ T(\delta)\}
\]
there is $C_{\nu}>0$ with $u(x)\le C_{\nu}|x|^{-\nu}$ for $|x|\ge1$.
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Since $\delta>\gamma_{e}$ we have $q\delta>q\gamma_{e}=N-\sigma/2>N-\sigma$, so Lemma \ref{lem:conv}(ii) applies with $\beta=\delta$ and gives, for $|x|\ge2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Wdecay}
W(x)\le C|x|^{-a'},
\qquad
a'=
\begin{cases}
a(\delta) &\text{if }q\delta\neq N,\\
\text{any } a'<\sigma &\text{if }q\delta=N,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where in the borderline case we absorbed the logarithm. Note that $W\in L^{\infty}$ by Lemma \ref{lem:Wbounded}, and that in all cases $a'$ may be taken arbitrarily close to $a(\delta)$ from below. Consequently the right hand side of \eqref{eq:main} obeys
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fdecay}
f:=Wu^{q-1}\le C|x|^{-m},
\qquad m:=a'+(q-1)\delta,
\qquad |x|\ge2 ,
\end{equation}
where we used $q>1$ and $u\le C_{\delta}|x|^{-\delta}$.

Fix $\nu<\min\{\gamma_{h},T(\delta)\}$ and, using \eqref{eq:Wdecay}, choose $a'$ so close to $a(\delta)$ that $\nu(p-1)+p\le m$. Set $\rho:=2$ and, on $\Omega:=\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus\overline{B_{\rho}}$, let $v(x):=M|x|^{-\nu}$. By \eqref{eq:barrier}, $h(\nu)>0$, and for $|x|\ge\rho$,
\[
-\Delta_{p}v=h(\nu)M^{p-1}|x|^{-\nu(p-1)-p}
\ge h(\nu)M^{p-1}\rho^{\,m-\nu(p-1)-p}|x|^{-m}
\ge C|x|^{-m}\ge f,
\]
provided $M$ is large enough, where we used $\nu(p-1)+p\le m$ in the first inequality. Enlarging $M$ further we also have $v\ge u$ on $\partial B_{\rho}$, because $u$ is bounded.

We check the assumptions of Lemma \ref{lem:comparison}. We may assume $\nu>\gamma_{e}$, since otherwise the asserted bound is weaker than the assumption $u\le C_{\delta}|x|^{-\delta}$ and there is nothing to prove. Both $u$ and $v$ lie in $W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\overline{\Omega})$, and $\nabla v\in L^{p}(\Omega)$ because
\[
\int_{\Omega}|\nabla v|^{p}\,dx\simeq\int_{\rho}^{\infty}r^{N-1-(\nu+1)p}\,dr<\infty
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad \nu>\gamma_{e}.
\]
Hence $(u-v)^{+}$ is dominated by $u\in D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$, vanishes on $\partial\Omega$, and has gradient $\nabla u-\nabla v\in L^{p}$ on the set where it is positive, so $(u-v)^{+}\in D^{1,p}(\Omega)$. Since $-\Delta_{p}u=f\le-\Delta_{p}v$ weakly in $\Omega$, Lemma \ref{lem:comparison} gives $u\le v$ in $\Omega$. Together with the boundedness of $u$ on $B_{\rho}$, this is the assertion.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
By Theorem \ref{thm:powergain}, there are $C>0$ and $\tau>0$ such that $u(x)\le C|x|^{-\gamma_{e}-\tau}$ for $|x|\ge1$. We may therefore start from $\delta_{0}:=\gamma_{e}+\tau>\gamma_{e}$. Fix
\[
\nu<\min\{\gamma_{h},\gamma_{*}\}.
\]
If $\nu\le\delta_{0}$, the conclusion follows immediately from Theorem \ref{thm:powergain}. We therefore assume $\delta_{0}<\nu$ and set
\[
F(\delta):=\min\{T(\delta),\gamma_{h}\}.
\]
As long as $\delta_{k}<\nu$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:strict-bootstrap}
\delta_{k+1}:=\frac{\delta_{k}+F(\delta_{k})}{2}.
\end{equation}
Since $\delta_{k}<\nu<\min\{\gamma_{h},\gamma_{*}\}$, Lemma \ref{lem:Tprops}(e) gives $T(\delta_{k})>\delta_{k}$, while also $\gamma_{h}>\delta_{k}$. Hence
\[
\delta_{k}<\delta_{k+1}<F(\delta_{k})=\min\{T(\delta_{k}),\gamma_{h}\}.
\]
Thus Lemma \ref{lem:onestep} applies at every step and, by induction, there is $C_{k}>0$ such that
\[
u(x)\le C_{k}|x|^{-\delta_{k}}\qquad\text{for }|x|\ge1 .
\]

It remains to show that $\delta_{k}\ge\nu$ for some finite $k$. Suppose otherwise. Then $(\delta_{k})$ is strictly increasing and bounded above by $\nu$, hence $\delta_{k}\to\delta_{\infty}$ for some
\[
\delta_{0}<\delta_{\infty}\le\nu<\min\{\gamma_{h},\gamma_{*}\}.
\]
Passing to the limit in \eqref{eq:strict-bootstrap} and using the continuity of $F$ gives $F(\delta_{\infty})=\delta_{\infty}$. Since $\delta_{\infty}<\gamma_{h}$, this implies $T(\delta_{\infty})=\delta_{\infty}$. By Lemma \ref{lem:Tprops}(d), the only positive fixed points of $T$ are $\gamma_{e}$ and $\gamma_{*}$, contradicting
$\gamma_{e}<\delta_{0}<\delta_{\infty}<\gamma_{*}$. Therefore some $\delta_{k}\ge\nu$, and the corresponding decay estimate proves the theorem.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:main}
Let $N\ge3$, $p>1$ and $2p<\sigma<N$, and assume that $q:=p^*_{\sigma}>1$ and $\Lambda>0$; the inequality $q>1$ follows automatically from Lemma \ref{lem:qbigger1}, and $\Lambda>0$ is automatic if $p\ge2$. Let $u$ be a nonnegative $D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$-weak solution of \eqref{eq:main}. Then $u\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$ for some $0<\alpha<\min\{1,\frac{1}{p-1}\}$, and either $u\equiv0$, or $u>0$ in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ and the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item The sharp asymptotic estimates \eqref{eq:sharp-u} and \eqref{eq:sharp-grad} hold.
\item The function $u$ is radially symmetric about some point $x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$ and strictly decreasing in the radial variable; equivalently, $u(x)=U(|x-x_{0}|)$ for a positive profile $U$ with $U'(r)<0$ for every $r>0$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:Vdecay}
Assume the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:main} and $u>0$. Then there are $\beta>p$, $C_{V}>0$ and $R_{2}\ge1$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Vdecay}
0\le V(x)\le C_{V}|x|^{-\beta}\qquad\text{for }|x|\ge R_{2},
\end{equation}
and $V\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap L^{N/p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
By Lemma \ref{lem:exponents}(c) we have $\gamma_{*}>\gamma_{h}$, so Theorem \ref{thm:bootstrap} provides, for every $\nu<\gamma_{h}$, a constant with $u\le C_{\nu}|x|^{-\nu}$ for $|x|\ge1$. Fix such a $\nu$, to be chosen. As in \eqref{eq:Wdecay}, Lemma \ref{lem:conv}(ii) gives $W\le C|x|^{-a'}$ for $|x|\ge2$ with $a'$ arbitrarily close to $a(\nu)=\min\{\sigma,q\nu+\sigma-N\}$ from below. By \eqref{eq:lower} and $q-p<0$,
\[
u^{q-p}(x)\le c_{1}^{q-p}|x|^{(p-q)\gamma_{h}}\qquad\text{for }|x|\ge R_{1},
\]
so \eqref{eq:Vdecay} holds with $\beta=a'-(p-q)\gamma_{h}$. Letting $\nu\uparrow\gamma_{h}$ and $a'\uparrow a(\nu)$ we get
\[
\beta\ \longrightarrow\ \min\{\sigma,\ q\gamma_{h}+\sigma-N\}-(p-q)\gamma_{h}
=\begin{cases}
p+\Lambda &\text{if }q\gamma_{h}>N,\\[2pt]
p+\dfrac{N+p-\sigma}{p-1} &\text{if }q\gamma_{h}\le N,
\end{cases}
\]
by Lemma \ref{lem:exponents}(d). In the first case the limit exceeds $p$ because $\Lambda>0$, and in the second because $\sigma<N$. Hence $\nu$ and $a'$ may be fixed so that $\beta>p$.

For the integrability, $V\le C_{K}W$ on every compact $K$ by Lemma \ref{lem:regularity}, and $W\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$ by Lemma \ref{lem:Wbounded}, so $V\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Consequently $\int_{B_{R_{2}}}V^{N/p}<\infty$, while by \eqref{eq:Vdecay},
\[
\int_{|x|\ge R_{2}}V^{N/p}\,dx\le C\int_{R_{2}}^{\infty}r^{N-1-\beta N/p}\,dr<\infty
\]
because $\beta>p$.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
By Lemma \ref{lem:regularity}, $u\in C^{1,\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}\cap L^{\infty}$ and either $u\equiv0$ or $u>0$. Assume $u>0$. By Proposition \ref{prop:Vdecay}, $u$ is a positive $D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$-weak solution of $-\Delta_{p}u=V(x)u^{p-1}$ with $0\le V\in L^{N/p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$, so \cite[Theorem 1.3]{DaiLiLiu2025} applies once its assumptions are checked.

The requirement $u\in L^{r}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for all $r<\infty$ holds because $u\in L^{\infty}$. The requirement $V\in L^{t}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for all $N/p<t<\infty$ holds because $V\in L^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}$, and it gives $u\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$.

We verify the decay property \cite[(1.9)]{DaiLiLiu2025} with constants uniform in the exponent $\bar p$, as required there. By Theorem \ref{thm:bootstrap} there are $\nu>\gamma_e$, $C_u>0$ and $R_u\ge1$ such that $u(x)\le C_u|x|^{-\nu}$ for $|x|\ge R_u$. Set
\[
\tau_0:=\frac{\nu-\gamma_e}{2}>0
\]
and choose $\widehat p$ so large that $\nu\widehat p>N$ and
$N/\widehat p\le(\nu-\gamma_e)/2$. Then, for every $\bar p\ge\widehat p$ and $R\ge R_u$,
\begin{align*}
\|u\|_{L^{\bar p}(\mathbb R^N\setminus B_{2R})}
&\le C_u\left(\frac{\omega_{N-1}}{\nu\bar p-N}\right)^{1/\bar p}(2R)^{-\nu+N/\bar p}\\
&\le C R^{-\gamma_e-\tau_0}
 =\frac{C}{R^{\frac{N-p}{p}+\tau_0}},
\end{align*}
where $C$ is independent of both $\bar p\ge\widehat p$ and $R\ge R_u$, because
$\sup_{\bar p\ge\widehat p}(\omega_{N-1}/(\nu\bar p-N))^{1/\bar p}<\infty$.
Thus \cite[(1.9)]{DaiLiLiu2025} holds in its stated uniform form. The uniform bound \cite[(1.10)]{DaiLiLiu2025} holds because \eqref{eq:Vdecay} gives $\|V\|_{L^{t}(B_{1}(x))}\le C|x|^{-\beta}\le C$ for $|x|\ge2R_{2}$. Finally the decay assumption on $V$ required for the two-sided estimates is exactly \eqref{eq:Vdecay} with $\beta>p$. Therefore \cite[Theorem 1.3]{DaiLiLiu2025} yields \eqref{eq:sharp-u} and \eqref{eq:sharp-grad}.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label{lem:nonlocal-diff}
Let $u$ be as in Theorem \ref{thm:main} with $u>0$, and let $x\in\Sigma_{\lambda}$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Wdiff}
W(x)-W(x_{\lambda})\le P_{\lambda}(x):=q\int_{\Sigma_{\lambda}}\frac{u^{q-1}(y)w(y)}{|x-y|^{\sigma}}\,dy .
\end{equation}
Moreover there are $C>0$ and $R_{3}\ge1$, independent of $\lambda\le0$, such that, on $\Sigma_{\lambda}\cap\{|x|\ge R_{3}\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Kbound}
W(x)\big(u^{q-1}(x)-u^{q-1}_{\lambda}(x)\big)\le K(x)\,w(x),
\qquad
K(x)\le\frac{C}{|x|^{\beta+(p-2)\gamma_{h}}},
\end{equation}
with $\beta>p$ as in Proposition \ref{prop:Vdecay}. More generally, for every $L>0$ the same estimate holds uniformly for $|\lambda|\le L$, after replacing $C,R_{3}$ by constants $C_{L},R_{3,L}$.
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Splitting $\mathbb{R}^{N}=\Sigma_{\lambda}\cup\Sigma_{\lambda}^{c}$ and changing variables $y\mapsto y_{\lambda}$ in the second part gives the classical identity
\[
W(x)-W(x_{\lambda})=\int_{\Sigma_{\lambda}}\Big(\frac{1}{|x-y|^{\sigma}}-\frac{1}{|x_{\lambda}-y|^{\sigma}}\Big)\big(u^{q}(y)-u^{q}_{\lambda}(y)\big)\,dy .
\]
For $x,y\in\Sigma_{\lambda}$ one has $|x-y|<|x_{\lambda}-y|$, so the kernel factor is positive and bounded by $|x-y|^{-\sigma}$. Dropping the part of the integral where $u\le u_{\lambda}$, which is nonpositive, and using $u^{q}-u^{q}_{\lambda}\le qu^{q-1}(u-u_{\lambda})$ on the remaining set, we obtain \eqref{eq:Wdiff}.

For \eqref{eq:Kbound}, the mean value theorem gives $u^{q-1}-u^{q-1}_{\lambda}\le(q-1)\zeta^{q-2}w$ with $\zeta$ between $u_{\lambda}(x)$ and $u(x)$, so $K=(q-1)W\zeta^{q-2}$. If $q\ge2$ then $\zeta^{q-2}\le u^{q-2}$, hence $K\le(q-1)Wu^{q-p}u^{p-2}=(q-1)Vu^{p-2}$, and \eqref{eq:sharp-u} together with \eqref{eq:Vdecay} gives $K\le C|x|^{-\beta-(p-2)\gamma_{h}}$. If $q<2$ then $\zeta^{q-2}\le u_{\lambda}^{q-2}(x)$. For $\lambda\le0$ and $x\in\Sigma_{\lambda}$ one has $|x_{\lambda}|\le|x|$, so the lower bound in \eqref{eq:sharp-u} gives
\[
u_{\lambda}(x)=u(x_{\lambda})\ge c_{0}(1+|x|^{\gamma_{h}})^{-1}.
\]
For the bounded-parameter version, if $|\lambda|\le L$ then $|x_{\lambda}|\le|x|+2L\le2|x|$ whenever $|x|\ge2L$, and the same lower estimate holds with a modified constant. Thus, uniformly either for all $\lambda\le0$ or for $|\lambda|\le L$ after increasing the radius,
$\zeta^{q-2}\le C|x|^{(2-q)\gamma_{h}}$. By \eqref{eq:Wdecay} with $a'$ as in Proposition \ref{prop:Vdecay},
\[
K\le C|x|^{-a'+(2-q)\gamma_{h}}=C|x|^{-\beta-(p-2)\gamma_{h}},
\]
because $\beta=a'-(p-q)\gamma_{h}$.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- lemma-26: body_md -->
\label{lem:I2}
With the notation above, for every $\lambda\in\mathbb R$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:I2}
\int_{\Sigma_{\lambda}}P_{\lambda}(x)u^{q-1}_{\lambda}(x)w(x)\,dx
\le C\|u\|^{2q-p^{*}}_{L^{p^{*}}(\Sigma_{\lambda})}\int_{\Sigma_{\lambda}}u^{p^{*}-2}w^{2}\,dx .
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-26 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Since $u_{\lambda}\le u$ on the support of $w$, the left hand side is bounded by
\[
q\int_{\Sigma_{\lambda}}\int_{\Sigma_{\lambda}}\frac{u^{q-1}(x)w(x)\,u^{q-1}(y)w(y)}{|x-y|^{\sigma}}\,dx\,dy
\le C\big\|u^{q-1}w\big\|^{2}_{L^{t}(\Sigma_{\lambda})},
\qquad t=\frac{2N}{2N-\sigma},
\]
by the Hardy--Littlewood--Sobolev inequality. Write $u^{q-1}w=u^{c}\cdot u^{(p^{*}-2)/2}w$ with $c:=q-p^{*}/2$, which is positive by Lemma \ref{lem:exponents}(a). H\"older's inequality with the exponents $A:=2N/(N-\sigma)$ and $2$, which satisfy $1/A+1/2=1/t$, gives
\[
\big\|u^{q-1}w\big\|_{L^{t}}\le\|u\|^{c}_{L^{cA}}\Big(\int_{\Sigma_{\lambda}}u^{p^{*}-2}w^{2}\Big)^{1/2},
\]
and a computation from \eqref{eq:psigma} shows $cA=p^{*}$. Since $2c=2q-p^{*}$, this is \eqref{eq:I2}. Note that the exponent $(p^{*}-2)/2$ is negative when $p<2N/(N+2)$, which does occur in the range \eqref{eq:new-range}; the H\"older step above is unaffected, and in the subsequent use of \eqref{eq:I2} the bound $u^{p^{*}-2}\le C|x|^{-(p^{*}-2)\gamma_{h}}$ is then furnished by the lower bound in \eqref{eq:sharp-u} rather than by the upper one.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- lemma-27: body_md -->
\label{lem:strict-separation}
Let $N\ge3$, $p>1$ and $2p<\sigma<N$, and assume that $q:=p^*_{\sigma}>1$. Let $u$ be a positive $D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$-weak solution of \eqref{eq:main}. Let $\lambda\in\mathbb R$ be such that $u\le u_\lambda$ in $\Sigma_\lambda$ and $u\not\equiv u_\lambda$ in $\Sigma_\lambda$. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item $W[u](x)<W[u](x_\lambda)$ for every $x\in\Sigma_\lambda$;
\item $u<u_\lambda$ almost everywhere in $\Sigma_\lambda$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-27 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
By Corollary \ref{cor:structure}, $u\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb R^N)$ and $\nabla u$ is bounded on compact sets.

(i) Splitting $\mathbb R^N$ into $\Sigma_\lambda$ and its complement and reflecting the second part, as in the proof of Lemma \ref{lem:nonlocal-diff},
\[
W[u](x)-W[u](x_\lambda)=\int_{\Sigma_\lambda}k_\lambda(x,y)\big(u^{q}(y)-u_\lambda^{q}(y)\big)\,dy,
\qquad
k_\lambda(x,y):=\frac{1}{|x-y|^{\sigma}}-\frac{1}{|x_\lambda-y|^{\sigma}},
\]
where all integrals are finite because $W[u]$ is finite everywhere. For $x,y\in\Sigma_\lambda$ one has $|x-y|<|x_\lambda-y|$, hence $k_\lambda(x,y)>0$. The factor $u^{q}-u_\lambda^{q}$ is nonpositive in $\Sigma_\lambda$ and, by continuity and $u\not\equiv u_\lambda$, strictly negative on a nonempty open subset of $\Sigma_\lambda$. Hence the integral is negative.

(ii) Put $v:=u_\lambda-u\ge0$ in $\Sigma_\lambda$ and $Z_0:=\{x\in\Sigma_\lambda:v(x)=0\}$. By the reflection invariance of $\Delta_p$, $u_\lambda$ is a weak solution of $-\Delta_pu_\lambda=W[u](x_\lambda)u_\lambda^{q-1}$ in $\Sigma_\lambda$. Set
\[
F:=|\nabla u_\lambda|^{p-2}\nabla u_\lambda-|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u,
\qquad
g(x):=W[u](x_\lambda)\,u_\lambda^{q-1}(x)-W[u](x)\,u^{q-1}(x),
\]
so that $\int_{\Sigma_\lambda}\langle F,\nabla\varphi\rangle=\int_{\Sigma_\lambda}g\varphi$ for every Lipschitz $\varphi$ with compact support in $\Sigma_\lambda$. On $Z_0$ one has $u=u_\lambda>0$, so by (i)
\[
g=\big(W[u](x_\lambda)-W[u](x)\big)u^{q-1}>0\qquad\text{on }Z_0 .
\]
Fix $0\le\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Sigma_\lambda)$ and, for $\varepsilon>0$, test with $\varphi=\eta\,\psi_\varepsilon(v)$, where $\psi_\varepsilon(t):=(1-t/\varepsilon)^{+}$. Since $\psi_\varepsilon'=-1/\varepsilon$ on $\{0\le v<\varepsilon\}$ and $\langle F,\nabla v\rangle\ge0$ by the monotonicity of $\xi\mapsto|\xi|^{p-2}\xi$,
\[
\int_{\Sigma_\lambda}g\,\eta\,\psi_\varepsilon(v)
=\int_{\Sigma_\lambda}\psi_\varepsilon(v)\langle F,\nabla\eta\rangle-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\{v<\varepsilon\}}\eta\,\langle F,\nabla v\rangle
\le\int_{\Sigma_\lambda}\psi_\varepsilon(v)\langle F,\nabla\eta\rangle .
\]
As $\varepsilon\to0$, $\psi_\varepsilon(v)\to\chi_{Z_0}$ pointwise, and $g$ and $F$ are bounded on the support of $\eta$, so dominated convergence gives
\[
\int_{Z_0}g\,\eta\le\int_{Z_0}\langle F,\nabla\eta\rangle=0 .
\]
The last equality holds because $\nabla v=0$ almost everywhere on the level set $Z_0$ by Stampacchia's theorem, so that $\nabla u=\nabla u_\lambda$ and $F=0$ almost everywhere on $Z_0$. Since $g>0$ on $Z_0$ and $\eta\ge0$ is arbitrary, $|Z_0|=0$.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- lemma-28: body_md -->
\label{lem:classical-weight}
Assume the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:main} and $u>0$. There are $\kappa\in(0,1]$, $c_w>0$ and $R_w\ge R_0$ such that, for every $\lambda\in\mathbb R$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:classical-weight}
|x_\lambda|\ge\kappa|x|
\quad\text{and}\quad
\rho_\lambda(x)\ge c_w|x|^{s}
\qquad\text{for }x\in A_\lambda,\ |x|\ge R_w .
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-28 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Let $x\in A_\lambda$ with $|x|$ large. Then $u(x)>u(x_\lambda)$, and \eqref{eq:sharp-u} gives $c_0(1+|x_\lambda|^{\gamma_h})^{-1}<C_0|x|^{-\gamma_h}$. Once $(c_0/(2C_0))|x|^{\gamma_h}\ge1$, this forces $|x_\lambda|^{\gamma_h}\ge(c_0/(2C_0))|x|^{\gamma_h}$, which is the first assertion with $\kappa=(c_0/(2C_0))^{1/\gamma_h}$. Note that no restriction on $\lambda$ was used. If $p\ge2$ then $s\le0$ and, by the lower bound in \eqref{eq:sharp-grad}, $\rho_\lambda(x)\ge|\nabla u(x)|^{p-2}\ge c_0^{p-2}|x|^{-(\gamma_h+1)(p-2)}=c_0^{p-2}|x|^{s}$. If $1<p<2$, the upper bound in \eqref{eq:sharp-grad} applied at $x$ and at $x_\lambda$, the latter with $|x_\lambda|\ge\kappa|x|\ge R_0$, gives $|\nabla u(x)|+|\nabla u(x_\lambda)|\le C|x|^{-\gamma_h-1}$, hence $\rho_\lambda(x)\ge c|x|^{s}$ because $p-2<0$. All constants are independent of $\lambda$.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Assume $u>0$, and write $w=(u-u_\lambda)^+$ and $\mathcal E_\lambda:=\int_{\Sigma_\lambda}\rho_\lambda|\nabla w|^2$. Testing \eqref{eq:weak} for $u$ and for $u_\lambda$ with the standard compactly supported cut-off approximations of $w$ in $\Sigma_\lambda$ (extended by zero across $T_\lambda$), subtracting, and then letting the outer cut-off tend to one, the vector inequality \cite[(2.10)]{DaiLiLiu2025}, the splitting \eqref{eq:splitting} and Lemma \ref{lem:nonlocal-diff} give, for a constant $C_1=C_1(p)>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:classical-energy}
C_1\mathcal E_\lambda
\le\int_{A_\lambda}K\,w^2+\int_{A_\lambda}P_\lambda\,u_\lambda^{q-1}w .
\end{equation}
Here $\mathcal E_\lambda<\infty$: with $M_\lambda:=\max\{|\nabla u|,|\nabla u_\lambda|\}$ one has $\rho_\lambda|\nabla w|^2\le 2^{\max\{p,2\}}M_\lambda^{p}$ for every $1<p<\infty$, since $|\nabla w|\le2M_\lambda$ and $\rho_\lambda\le M_\lambda^{p-2}$ if $p<2$, $\rho_\lambda\le(2M_\lambda)^{p-2}$ if $p\ge2$. So the integrand is bounded, and by \eqref{eq:sharp-grad} and \eqref{eq:classical-weight} it is $O(|x|^{-p(\gamma_h+1)})$ on $A_\lambda$ at infinity, which is integrable because $p(\gamma_h+1)-N=p(\gamma_h-\gamma_e)>0$.

We use three pointwise bounds on $A_\lambda\cap\{|x|\ge R\}$, valid for $R$ large, uniformly for $\lambda\le0$ and, with constants depending on $L$, for $|\lambda|\le L$; only the first one depends on $L$, through Lemma \ref{lem:nonlocal-diff}. First, \eqref{eq:Kbound} together with the identity $-\beta-(p-2)\gamma_h=s-2-(\beta-p)$ gives $K\le C|x|^{s-2}|x|^{-(\beta-p)}$. Second, since $u\simeq|x|^{-\gamma_h}$ by \eqref{eq:sharp-u}, $u^{p^*-2}\le C|x|^{s-2}|x|^{-\beta_2}$ with $\beta_2:=(p^*-2)\gamma_h+s-2>0$. Third, $|x|^{s}\le c_w^{-1}\rho_\lambda$ by \eqref{eq:classical-weight}. Moreover $\beta>p$ by Proposition \ref{prop:Vdecay}, $s>2-N$, and \eqref{eq:cutoff-hardy} with $\ell=0$ applies to $w$ by the discussion following it.

\emph{Step 1: the plane starts.} For $\lambda\le-R_w$ one has $\Sigma_\lambda\subset\{|x|>|\lambda|\}$. Applying \eqref{eq:log-hardy} with $\ell=0$ and $R=|\lambda|$ to $w$ extended by zero, and then Lemma \ref{lem:I2},
\[
\int_{A_\lambda}Kw^2+\int_{A_\lambda}P_\lambda u_\lambda^{q-1}w
\le C\big(|\lambda|^{-(\beta-p)}+\|u\|^{2q-p^*}_{L^{p^*}(\Sigma_\lambda)}|\lambda|^{-\beta_2}\big)\Big(\frac{2}{N+s-2}\Big)^2c_w^{-1}\mathcal E_\lambda .
\]
For $|\lambda|$ large the coefficient is smaller than $C_1$, so \eqref{eq:classical-energy} forces $\mathcal E_\lambda=0$. If $1<p<2$, this gives $\nabla w=0$ a.e. on the complement of the common critical set, while $\nabla w=0$ also on the common critical set itself. If $p\ge2$, the critical set of $u$ has measure zero by \cite[Corollary 2.10, Remark 2.11]{DaiLiLiu2025}, hence $\rho_\lambda>0$ a.e. and again $\nabla w=0$ a.e. Since $w$ vanishes on $T_\lambda$ and at infinity, $w\equiv0$. Thus $u\le u_\lambda$ in $\Sigma_\lambda$ for all $\lambda$ sufficiently negative.

\emph{Step 2: the plane reaches a symmetry position.} Let $\lambda_0:=\sup\{\lambda:u\le u_\mu\text{ in }\Sigma_\mu\text{ for every }\mu\le\lambda\}$. It is finite, since $u>0$ tends to zero at infinity, and $u\le u_{\lambda_0}$ in $\Sigma_{\lambda_0}$ by continuity. Suppose $u\not\equiv u_{\lambda_0}$ in $\Sigma_{\lambda_0}$. By Lemma \ref{lem:strict-separation}, $u<u_{\lambda_0}$ a.e. in $\Sigma_{\lambda_0}$. Since $u_\lambda\to u_{\lambda_0}$ pointwise as $\lambda\to\lambda_0$, every such point lies outside $A_\lambda$ for $\lambda$ close to $\lambda_0$, while $|(\Sigma_\lambda\setminus\Sigma_{\lambda_0})\cap B_{3R}|\to0$. Dominated convergence of characteristic functions therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:classical-measure-small}
|A_\lambda\cap B_{3R}|\longrightarrow0\qquad\text{as }\lambda\downarrow\lambda_0,\ \text{for every fixed }R .
\end{equation}
Put $L:=|\lambda_0|+1$ and let $\lambda\in(\lambda_0,\lambda_0+1]$. Split both integrals in \eqref{eq:classical-energy} at $|x|=2R$ and set
\[
X_\lambda:=\int_{A_\lambda\cap B_{2R}}w^2 .
\]
On $B_{2R}$ the functions $K$ and $u^{p^*-2}$ are bounded uniformly in $\lambda$, because $u$ and $u_\lambda$ are bounded above and below by positive constants there. On $|x|\ge2R$ we use the three pointwise bounds and \eqref{eq:cutoff-hardy} with $\ell=0$; its annular error is bounded by $C_RX_\lambda$. This gives
\[
C_1\mathcal E_\lambda\le C\big(R^{-(\beta-p)}+R^{-\beta_2}\big)c_w^{-1}\mathcal E_\lambda+C_RX_\lambda .
\]
We now fix $R$ so large that the first coefficient is at most $C_1/2$; the constants depend only on $L$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:classical-reduced}
\tfrac12C_1\mathcal E_\lambda\le C_RX_\lambda .
\end{equation}
It remains to show $X_\lambda\le\theta_\lambda\mathcal E_\lambda$ with $\theta_\lambda\to0$ as $\lambda\downarrow\lambda_0$.

If $1<p<2$, let $G:=\|\nabla u\|_{L^\infty(\mathbb R^N)}<\infty$. On the set where $|\nabla u|+|\nabla u_\lambda|>0$ one has $\rho_\lambda\ge(2G)^{p-2}$, while on the common critical set $\nabla w=0$. Hence
\[
\int_{\Sigma_\lambda}|\nabla w|^2\le(2G)^{2-p}\mathcal E_\lambda.
\]
Since $w\in D^{1,2}(\Sigma_\lambda)$ by the discussion following \eqref{eq:cutoff-hardy}, H\"older's and Sobolev's inequalities give
\[
X_\lambda\le|A_\lambda\cap B_{2R}|^{2/N}\Big(\int_{\Sigma_\lambda}w^{2^*}\Big)^{2/2^*}\le C(2G)^{2-p}|A_\lambda\cap B_{2R}|^{2/N}\mathcal E_\lambda .
\]

If $p\ge2$, let $\xi\in C^\infty_c(B_{3R})$ with $0\le\xi\le1$, $\xi=1$ on $B_{2R}$ and $|\nabla\xi|\le2/R$, and put $\varrho:=|\nabla u|^{p-2}$. Let $r_R>2$ be furnished by Lemma \ref{lem:weighted-sobolev-local} on $B_{3R}$ and set $\delta_R:=1-2/r_R>0$. Since $\xi w$ is supported in $A_\lambda\cap B_{3R}$, H\"older's inequality followed by Lemma \ref{lem:weighted-sobolev-local} gives
\[
X_\lambda\le\int_{A_\lambda\cap B_{3R}}(\xi w)^2
\le |A_\lambda\cap B_{3R}|^{\delta_R}\|\xi w\|_{L^{r_R}(B_{3R})}^2
\le C_R|A_\lambda\cap B_{3R}|^{\delta_R}\int_{B_{3R}}\varrho|\nabla(\xi w)|^2.
\]
Because $\varrho\le\rho_\lambda$ for $p\ge2$ and $\varrho$ is bounded on $B_{3R}$,
\[
X_\lambda\le C_R|A_\lambda\cap B_{3R}|^{\delta_R}
\big(\mathcal E_\lambda+Y_\lambda\big),
\qquad
Y_\lambda:=\int_{A_\lambda\cap(B_{3R}\setminus B_{2R})}w^2.
\]
Since $s-2<0$, one has $|x|^{s-2}\ge(3R)^{s-2}$ on the annulus, and \eqref{eq:cutoff-hardy} with the third pointwise bound gives $Y_\lambda\le C_R(\mathcal E_\lambda+X_\lambda)$. Therefore
\[
X_\lambda\le C_R|A_\lambda\cap B_{3R}|^{\delta_R}(\mathcal E_\lambda+X_\lambda).
\]
By \eqref{eq:classical-measure-small}, the coefficient tends to zero as $\lambda\downarrow\lambda_0$; for $\lambda$ sufficiently close to $\lambda_0$ it can be absorbed, yielding $X_\lambda\le o(1)\mathcal E_\lambda$.

In both cases $\theta_\lambda\to0$ by \eqref{eq:classical-measure-small}. Inserting into \eqref{eq:classical-reduced} gives $\mathcal E_\lambda=0$ for $\lambda-\lambda_0>0$ small, hence $w\equiv0$ as in Step 1, that is $u\le u_\lambda$ in $\Sigma_\lambda$. This contradicts the definition of $\lambda_0$. Therefore $u\equiv u_{\lambda_0}$ in $\Sigma_{\lambda_0}$.

\emph{Step 3: conclusion.} Since the direction was arbitrary, $u$ is symmetric with respect to a hyperplane orthogonal to every direction, hence radially symmetric about some point $x_0$. Writing $u(x)=U(|x-x_0|)$ with $U\in C^1([0,\infty))$, the function $f=W[u]u^{q-1}$ is radial about $x_0$ and positive, and testing the equation with radial functions gives
\[
r^{N-1}|U'(r)|^{p-2}U'(r)=-\int_0^r t^{N-1}f(t)\,dt<0\qquad(r>0),
\]
the boundary term at $r=0$ vanishing because $U'$ is bounded. Hence $U'(r)<0$ for every $r>0$. This proves Theorem \ref{thm:main}(ii).
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
The only structural input of the moving planes argument that distinguishes the present range from that of \cite{DaiLiLiu2025} is the monotonicity of $t\mapsto t^{q-1}$, that is, $q>1$, which is supplied by Lemma \ref{lem:qbigger1}. The exponent $p$ plays no role in the sign of either term of \eqref{eq:splitting}.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:qbigger1-bad}
Unlike in Theorem \ref{thm:main}, the condition $q>1$ is a genuine assumption in Theorems \ref{thm:structure} and \ref{thm:slowrate}. At the resonant boundary it is automatic, because $\Lambda=0$ gives
\[
q-1=\frac{N-\sigma}{\gamma_h}>0.
\]
In the strict slow phase, $q\le1$ occurs exactly when $\sigma\ge2N-2N/p+2$, in which case the local factor $u^{q-1}$ becomes singular and \eqref{eq:main} is a different problem. Both nonclassical regimes are nonempty, and both require $p<2N/(N+2)$, since $\Lambda\le0$ forces $\sigma\ge2p/(2-p)$ and $\sigma<N$. For $\Lambda<0$, take $N=10$, $p=3/2$ and $\sigma=7$, which give $q=39/34$, $\gamma_e<\gamma_*<\gamma_h$ and $q\gamma_*>N$. For $\Lambda=0$ with the same $N$ and $p$, take $\sigma=2p/(2-p)=6$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- lemma-29: body_md -->
\label{lem:Wsat}
Let $q>1$ and let $u$ be a positive $D^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{N})$-weak solution of \eqref{eq:main}. Suppose that for some $\delta_{\sharp}>0$,
\[
u(x)\le C_{\sharp}|x|^{-\delta_{\sharp}}\qquad(|x|\ge1),
\qquad q\delta_{\sharp}>N.
\]
Then $m_{0}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u^{q}\,dy$ is finite and positive, and there are $A_{1},A_{2}>0$ and $R_{5}\ge2$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Wsat-two}
A_{1}|x|^{-\sigma}\le W(x)\le A_{2}|x|^{-\sigma}
\qquad\text{for }|x|\ge R_{5}.
\end{equation}
One may take
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Wsat-low}
A_{1}:=\Big(\frac{2}{3}\Big)^{\sigma}\frac{m_{0}}{2}.
\end{equation}
